The fermionic DGFF and its scaling limit logCFT
David Adame - Carrillo, Wioletta M. Ruszel

Abstract
In this paper, we identify the scaling limit of the fermionic discrete Gaussian free field (fDGFF) as a logarithmic conformal field theory (CFT) in two dimensions. We first establish a one-to-one correspondence between the space of local observables of the fDGFF and the space of local fields of the symplectic fermions CFT, a logarithmic CFT with central charge c = − 2 c=-2 . This correspondence is meaningful in the sense that, when appropriately renormalised, the fDGFF correlation functions converge to corresponding CFT correlation functions in the scaling limit. As an application to these results, we interpret (the scaling limit of) certain local observables in the uniform spanning tree and the Abelian sandpile model as local fields of the symplectic fermions.



Keywords:
fermionic Gaussian free field,
symplectic fermions,
conformal field theory,
local fields, Virasoro structure,
discrete complex analysis,
scaling limit of corelation functions,
uniform spanning tree,
Abelian sandpile model.

1Introduction

Lattice models and (logarithmic) conformal field theory.

In the past twenty-five years, substantial progress has been made in the rigorous understanding of (the scaling limit of) critical models of statistical mechanics in the framework of conformal field theory (CFT) in two dimensions.
Notable milestones include Kenyon’s analysis of the height function of the dimer model —
[
Ken00
,
Ken01
]
— as well as the works of Chelkak, Hongler, Izyurov and Smirnov on the Ising model —
[
Smi10
,
Hon10
,
ChSm12
,
HoSm13
,
CHI15
,
CHI21
]
—.
In those works, the authors identify the scaling limit of the —suitable renormalised— correlation functions of certain observables in the corresponding lattice model as CFT correlation functions.
These achievements constituted rigorous confirmation of a prediction made by the physicists Belavin, Polyakov and Zamolodchikov in the 1980s, asserting that probabilistic models at criticality can be described by conformal field theories —
[
BPZ84a
,
BPZ84b
]
—.
Despite the extensive body of work in the Physics literature inspired by this conjecture, most models of statistical mechanics continue to lack a complete rigorous treatment of their scaling-limit CFT picture.

In the breakthrough work
[
HKV22
]
, the authors address this conjecture from a new angle in the context of the Ising model and the discrete Gaussian free field.
The key insight is the following:
employing techniques of discrete complex analysis,
the arguments typically used in the CFT literature can be reproduced at the discrete level in models that are sufficiently integrable.
This way, one can render the space of local observables of the discrete model a representation of the relevant symmetry algebra.
This approach permits a more systematic and exhaustive analysis of the space of local observables of a discrete model.
This program was further applied to fermionic observables of the double-dimer model in
[
Ada25
]
.
Furthermore, in
[
ABK24
]
, the authors used this approach to establish a one-to-one correspondence between local observables of the DGFF and local fields in the free boson CFT—hence finding for the first time the full structure of a CFT in a lattice model.

In certain critical models of statistical mechanics, the correlation functions
of specific observables exhibit logarithmic divergencies when their insertion points are brought together.
While this behaviour appears to spoil the conformal covariance of the theory, it can still be incorporated in the framework of CFT—theories featuring this phenomenon are referred to as logarithmic conformal field theories (logCFTs).
From a rigorous perspective, this class of models has recently attracted considerable attention.
In particular, Ruelle and others found natural observables in the Abelian sandpile model that can be accommodated in a logCFT —see
[
PoRu17
]
; see also
[
Rue21
]
for a review—
and Camia and Feng have made substantial progress on the description of critical (site Bernoulli) percolation as a logarithmic CFT.

One of the most well-understood examples of a logCFT is the symplectic fermions, which was first described by Kausch in
[
Kau00
]
—see
[
Ada24
]
for a review of the theory in general domains.
This logCFT has been connected to the uniform spanning tree —
[
LPW25
]
— and the Abelian sandpile model —
[
Rue21
]
—.
In turn, certain correlation functions in these two probabilistic models are well-known to be computable via a discrete model known as the fermionic discrete Gaussian free field (fDGFF)—see, e.g.,
[
CCRR23
]
.

For a given mesh size
\delta>0
, the fermionic DGFF on a discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}\subset\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
can be roughly understood as generated by two fermionic fiedlds
\xi
and
\theta
whose correlation functions are discrete harmonic as long as the fields are inserted away from each other.
The fermionic nature of the fields yields a determinantal structure of the multipoint correlation functions of the model.
See Section
3.1
for the precise definition.

Contribution and discussion.

In this paper, inspired by
[
HKV22
,
ABK24
]
, we aim at conceiving the fermionic discrete Gaussian free field as a discretised version of the symplectic fermions conformal field theory.
The way this statement is made precise is by establishing a one-to-one correspondence between local observables of the fDGFF and local fields in the CFT such that —when appropriately renormalised— the discrete correlation functions of the former converge to the CFT correlation functions of the latter in the scaling limit.

Our first main result concerning the discrete-local-observable/CFT-local-field is stated algebraically.
To that end, let us briefly review some algebraic facts of two-dimensional CFT.
The conformal symmetries of the theory are encoded into the space of fields by the Virasoro algebra,
that is, the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra

\displaystyle\textnormal{{Vir}}\,\coloneqq\,\Big(\,\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathbb{C}\,\textnormal{L}_{n}\Big)\oplus\mathbb{C}\,\textnormal{C}

equipped with the Lie brackets

\displaystyle\big[\textnormal{L}_{n},\textnormal{L}_{m}\big]\,=\,(n-m)\,\textnormal{L}_{n+m}+\delta_{n+m}\frac{n^{3}-n}{12}\,\textnormal{C}\,,\mspace{70mu}\big[\textnormal{C},\textnormal{L}_{n}\big]\,=\,0,

for all
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In particular, the space of fields of a two-dimensional CFT is a representation of (two commuting copies of) the Virasoro algebra.
In CFT, the central element
C
acts as a fixed scalar
c
multiple of the identity operator—in the model at hand, we have
c=-2
.

A characteristic feature of logCFTs is their more exotic Virasoro structure; unlike in standard QFT, the energy operator
(\textnormal{L}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\textnormal{L}$}}}}\textnormal{L}_{0})
is not diagonalisable.
Geometrically, the operator
(\textnormal{L}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\textnormal{L}$}}}}\textnormal{L}_{0})
is the generator of dilations—its (generalised) eigenvalues are called (generalised) scaling dimensions and they determine the behaviour of a given field under rescalings.

On the other hand, in the discrete setting, one should think of local observables of the fDGFF as polynomials on the fields
\xi
and
\theta
and their derivatives at a point.
However, at any given positive mesh size
\delta>0
, these polynomials involve the field values on a finite neighbourhood of the insertion point—see Section
3.2
for their definition.
This space can be rendered a
\textnormal{{Vir}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{Vir}}}
representation adapting the techniques developed in
[
Ada25
,
ABK24
]
.
Then, our first main result can be informally stated as follows.

Theorem 1.

As representations of
\textnormal{{Vir}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{Vir}}}
, we have the isomorphism

\displaystyle\bigg\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{local observables of}\\
\text{the fermionic DGFF}\end{array}\bigg\}\;\cong\;\bigg\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{local fields of the}\\
\text{symplectic fermions}\end{array}\bigg\}\,.

More precisely, we prove that those spaces are isomorphic as representations of the symplectic fermion symmetry algebra, which implies the above statement.
See Theorem
4.1
for the precise statement.

The second main result concerns the scaling limit of correlation functions.
The definition of the discrete correlation functions of the fermionic DGFF can be found in Section
3.1
;
the CFT correlation functions are defined in Section
2.1.2
.

The setup of the scaling limit is as follows.
Let
\Omega^{\delta}\subset\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
be discrete domains that approximate —in an appropriate sense— the simply-connected domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}
as
\delta\downarrow 0
, and let
z_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,z_{n}^{\delta}
be the points in
\Omega^{\delta}
that are closest to the fixed points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\Omega
respectively.
We also fix a positive constant
\lambda>0
, whose physicsl interpretation we shall discuss after the statement of the theorem.
Our second main result can then be stated informally as follows.

Theorem 2.

Let
\bm{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\varphi}_{n}
be symplectic-fermions local fields with generalised scaling dimensions
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
respectively.
Let
F_{i}
and
\tilde{F}_{i}
be the fDGFF local observables corresponding —via Theorem 1— to the local field
\bm{\varphi}_{i}
and
[(\textnormal{L}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\textnormal{L}$}}}}\textnormal{L}_{0})-D_{i}]\bm{\varphi}_{i}
, respectively.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Big\langle\Big(F_{1}(z^{\delta}_{1})-\log(\lambda\delta)\tilde{F}_{1}(z^{\delta}_{1})\Big)\!\cdots\!\Big(F_{n}(z^{\delta}_{n})-\log(\lambda\delta)\tilde{F}_{n}(z^{\delta}_{n})\Big)\Big\rangle^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}{{{\delta}}^{\,D_{1}}\;\cdots\;{{\delta}}^{\,D_{n}}}\mspace{4mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{20mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{20mu}}\mspace{1mu}\Big\langle\bm{\varphi}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots\bm{\varphi}_{n}(z_{n})\Big\rangle^{\textnormal{SyFe}}_{\Omega;\lambda}

uniformly for
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
on compacts of
\Omega
and away from one another.

The constant
\lambda>0
should be interpreted as an arbitrary choice of a linear scale—there is no good reason to pick the square side length
\delta
instead of the square diagonal length
\sqrt{2}\delta
as the characteristic linear scale of our approximation
\Omega^{\delta}
.
This fact leads to the scaling limit not being unique, but rather unique up to a choice of a linear scale.
Indeed, notice that we added a dependence on
\lambda
in the CFT correlation functions.
This non-uniqueness is explained in CFT by the existence of non-trivial self-isomorphism of the space of fields of the symplectic fermions;
see
[
Ada24
]
for more details.
We give explicit examples of this phenomenon in Example
2.5
and Section
4.3.2
.

These results have applications to the the uniform spanning tree (UST) and the Abelian sandpile model (ASM) via their connection to the fermionic DGFF.
In particular, any local observable in those models whose correlation functions can be computed with fDGFF correlation functions can be identified with a local field in the symplectic fermions CFT.
Moreover, the (appropriately renormalised) scaling limit of its correlation functions can be explicitly computed.
In Section
4.3
, we discuss these matters for the following observables:
the degree field and the open-horizontal-edge field in the UST,
and the height-one field and the dissipation field in the ASM.

Organisation of the paper.

In Section
2
, we provide the specific background that is required for the precise formulation of our results.
In particular, we discuss the symplectic fermions CFT and present the tools of discrete complex analysis that we employ in our treatment.
Section
3
is devoted to the definition of the fDGFF and its space of local fields.
Furthermore, we define certain operators —the current modes— in the space of local fields that are key in our approach.
Our two main results —Theorems 1 and 2 above— are stated in precise terms and discussed in Section
4
.
We complement these results with concrete applications to two probabilistic models—namely, the Abelian sandpile model and the uniform spanning tree.
Most proofs throughout the text are postponed to Section
5
.

project 346309: Finnish Centre of Excellence in Randomness and Structures (FiRSt).

2Background

In this section, we review notions that, while possibly familiar to some readers, are essential in the derivation of our results.
In addition, this section is used to establish the notation used throughout the remainder of the text.

In Section
2.1
we review the symplectic fermions CFT, this is based on
[
Ada24
]
.
In particular, in Section
2.1.1
, we give an explicit construction of its space of local fields as the logarithmic Fock space and, in Section
2.1.2
, we characterise its correlation functions on general domains of the complex plane.
The tools of discrete complex analysis that we use in our analysis are presented in Section
2.2
.

2.1The symplectic fermions CFT

The symplectic fermions is a conformal field theory that possesses a symmetry algebra that is more elementary than the Virasoro algebra.
Its symmetry algebra we call the
symplectic fermions algebra
—it is the vector space

\displaystyle\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}\,\coloneqq\,\left(\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{C}}\,{\upeta}_{k}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{C}}\,{\upchi}_{k}\right)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathsf{k}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{k}\right)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{k}$}}}}\mathsf{k}

equipped with the Lie superbrackets

\displaystyle\big\{{\upeta}_{k},{\upchi}_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,k\,\delta_{k+\ell}\,\mathsf{k}\,,\phantom{\Big|}\mspace{100mu}\big\{{\upeta}_{k},{\upeta}_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,\big\{{\upchi}_{k},{\upchi}_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,

\displaystyle\,0\,=\,\big[\,\mathsf{k},\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}\,\big]\,,

\displaystyle\phantom{\bigg|}\big\{\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,k\,\delta_{k+\ell}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{k}$}}}}\mathsf{k}\,,\phantom{\Big|}\mspace{100mu}\big\{\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,\big\{\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,

\displaystyle\,0\,=\,\big[\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{k}$}}}}\mathsf{k},\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}\,\big]\,,

and

\displaystyle\big\{{\upchi}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,\big\{{\upchi}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,\big\{{\upeta}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,\big\{{\upeta}_{k},

\displaystyle\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{\ell}\big\}\,=\,0

for
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.

2.1.1.Space of fields

The space of fields of the theory is constructed as a Fock space of the symmetry algebra.
We first consider the associative algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{SF}\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 1.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathcal{SF}$}}}}\mathcal{SF}\,=\,\mathcal{U}\big(\,\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}\,\big)\,\big/\,(\,\mathsf{k}-1\,,\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{k}$}}}}\mathsf{k}-1\,)

where the generators
\mathsf{k}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{k}$}}}}\mathsf{k}
are set to
1
.
The
logarithmic Fock space
of the symplectic fermions is then defined as the quotient

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}\,\coloneqq\,\mathcal{SF}\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 1.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathcal{SF}$}}}}\mathcal{SF}\,\big/\,\big(\,{\upeta}_{0}-\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{0}\,,\;{\upchi}_{0}-\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{0}\,,\;{\upeta}_{k}\,,\;{\upchi}_{k}\,,\;\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}\,,\;\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{k}\,\colon\,k>0\,\big)

of the associative algebra
\mathcal{SF}\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 1.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathcal{SF}$}}}}\mathcal{SF}
by the left ideal generated by
{\upeta}_{k}
,
{\upchi}_{k}
,
\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{k}
with positive indices
k
, and the elements
{\upeta}_{0}-\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{0}
and
{\upchi}_{0}-\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{0}
.
Note that
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
is canonically a representation of
\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}
and a (
\mathcal{SF}\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 1.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathcal{SF}$}}}}\mathcal{SF}
)-module.

The logarithmic Fock space serves as the space of local fields of the symplectic fermions CFT; in Section
2.1.2
below we will construct the correlation functions of this theory.
There is a number of fields that play distinguished roles in the construction.
First, we have the
ground states
.
These are the bosonic states

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\,

\displaystyle\,\coloneqq\,[\,{\upchi}_{0}{\upeta}_{0}\,]\,,

(
identity field
)

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\omega}\,

\displaystyle\,\coloneqq\,[\,1\,]\,,

(
logarithmic partner
of
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
)

and the
ground fermions

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}\,\coloneqq\,-[\,{\upeta}_{0}\,]\,,\mspace{50mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{50mu}\bm{\xi}\,\coloneqq\,-[\,{\upchi}_{0}\,]\,.

Further, we have the
holomorphic currents

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}\,\coloneqq\,{\upchi}_{-1}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\,,\mspace{50mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{50mu}\bm{\eta}\,\coloneqq\,{\upeta}_{-1}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\,,

and the
antiholomorphic currents

\displaystyle\overline{\bm{\chi}}\,\coloneqq\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-1}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\,,\mspace{50mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{50mu}\overline{\bm{\eta}}\,\coloneqq\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-1}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\,.

The vector space
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
admits the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt-type basis

\displaystyle{\upeta}_{-k_{r}}\cdots{\upeta}_{-k_{2}}{\upeta}_{-k_{1}}{\upchi}_{-\ell_{s}}\cdots{\upchi}_{-\ell_{2}}{\upchi}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-\bar{k}_{2}}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-\bar{k}_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-\bar{\ell}_{2}}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}}\bm{\omega}

(2.1)

with

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}r,s,\bar{r},\bar{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

and the orderings

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0\leq k_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots<k_{r}}\,,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\cdots<\ell_{s}}\,,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0<\bar{k}_{1}<\bar{k}_{2}<\cdots<\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\,,\text{ and }

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0<\bar{\ell}_{1}<\bar{\ell}_{2}<\cdots<\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\,.

Remark 2.1
.

Although the space
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
is not an irreducible representation of
\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}
, it is cyclically generated by the action of the generators of
\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}
on the vector
\bm{\omega}
.
From the explicit basis (
2.1
) and the anticommutation relations —Lie superbrackets— of the generators,
one can argue that the only proper subrepresentations of
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
are the ones cyclically generated by the other ground states —
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
,
\bm{\xi}
, and
\bm{\theta}
—.

\diamond

The logarithmic Fock space can be rendered a Virasoro algebra representation via the so-called Sugawara construction.
That is the object of the following lemma, which can be found as a special case of
[
Kac98
, Proposition 5.1]
; a computational proof can be found in
[
Ada25
, Theorem 5.2]
.
Let
[\,\cdot,\cdot\,]
denote the usual commutator of linear operators, that is
[A,B]\coloneqq AB-BA
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The linear operators on
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
given by the formal sums

\displaystyle{\mathsf{L}}_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k\,\geq\,n/2}{\upchi}_{n-k}{\upeta}_{k}-\sum_{k\,<\,n/2}{\upeta}_{k}{\upchi}_{n-k}\mspace{30mu}\text{ and }\mspace{30mu}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k\,\geq\,n/2}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{n-k}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}-\sum_{k\,<\,n/2}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{n-k}\,,

for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, are well-defined and constitute two commuting representations of the Virasoro algebra with central charge
-2
.
That is, they satisfy

\displaystyle\,\big[\,{\mathsf{L}}_{n},{\mathsf{L}}_{m}\big]\,=\,(n-m)\,{\mathsf{L}}_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}\,(n^{3}-n)\,\delta_{n+m}\,\textnormal{id}_{\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}}\,,

\displaystyle\,\big[\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{n},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{m}\big]\,=\,(n-m)\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}\,(n^{3}-n)\,\delta_{n+m}\,\textnormal{id}_{\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}}\,,\mspace{20mu}\text{and}

\displaystyle\,\big[\,{\mathsf{L}}_{n},\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{m}\big]\,=\,0

with
c=-2
.

The operators
{\mathsf{L}}_{n}
are called
holomorphic Virasoro modes
and the operators
\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{n}
are called
antiholomorphic Virasoro modes
.

2.1.2.Correlation functions

In a field theory, the physically significant quantities are correlation functions.
Given
{n\geq 1}
fields
\bm{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\varphi}_{n}
in a field theory, their correlation function is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-valued multivariable function denoted by

\displaystyle(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\longmapsto\Big\langle\bm{\varphi}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots\bm{\varphi}_{n}(z_{n})\Big\rangle\,

where the points
z_{i}
live in some ambient space where the theory takes place.
In this section, we present the characterisation of these functions for the symplectic fermions CFT in general domains of
{\mathbb{C}}
—we refer the reader to
[
Ada24
]
for a more detailed discussion.
For the rest of the paper, we take
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}
to be a (non-empty) proper, simply-connected open subset of the complex plane.

A notable feature of this theory is the existence of a family —parametrised by a complex number
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
— of non-trivial isomorphisms
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}\to\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
.
This fact suggests that the correlation functions of the theory cannot be unique, but rather unique up to the action of such isomorphism on the space of fields.
Although this fact seems a priori burdensome, we argue that it is a distinctive and celebrated property of logarithmic theories—we will see the necessity of this ambiguity when considering scaling limits in Section
4.2
, in which an arbitrary choice of scale appears naturally.
An instance of this feature will be discussed in the context of a particular probabilistic model —the Abelian sandpile— in Example
4.13
.

Fixing a constant
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the
correlation functions
of the symplectic fermions are a collection of linear maps

\displaystyle\Big\{\;\big\langle\cdots\big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\;\colon\;\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}^{\,\otimes n}\longrightarrow C^{\,\omega}\big(\textnormal{Conf}_{n}(\Omega),{\mathbb{C}}\big)\,\Big\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}}\,,

where
C^{\,\omega}\big(\textnormal{Conf}_{n}(\Omega),{\mathbb{C}}\big)
is the set of
{\mathbb{C}}
-valued real-analytic functions on the
n
-point configuration space

\displaystyle\textnormal{Conf}_{n}(\Omega)\coloneqq\Big\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\Omega^{n}\,\big|\,z_{i}\neq z_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j\Big\}\,

of the domain
\Omega
.

The correlation functions of the symplectic fermions can be obtained following a bootstrap procedure—this is exploited in the Theorem
2.3
below to characterise them.
The idea of the bootstrap approach is to rely on general consistency conditions that are valid for general two-dimensional CFTs —rather than on a specific Lagrangian— to construct the correlation functions of the theory.
To start the bootstrap approach, we fix the correlation functions of the ground states.
In particular, following the dictates of CFT —see
[
Ada24
]
for more details— a sensitive choice is

\displaystyle\Big\langle\bm{\xi}(z)\bm{\theta}(w)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,4\pi\,\mathbf{G}_{\Omega}(z,w)\,,

where
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega}
is Green’s function of the laplacian operator
\Delta=\partial^{2}_{x}+\partial^{2}_{y}
with Dirichlet boundary conditions.
We take the normalisation of
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega}
such that we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\Omega}(z,w)\,=\,-\frac{\log|\,z-w\,|}{2\pi}+\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,w)

with
\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}\colon\Omega^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
harmonic on both variables separately.
Then, the rest of correlation functions can be obtained by identifying the fields that carry the symplectic-fermion algebraic structure in their Fourier modes.
These fields are the currents.

In what follows, we use the Wirtinger derivatives
\partial
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial
.
That is, for a function of complex variable
z=x+\mathbbm{i}y
, with
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\partial_{z}=\partial_{x}-\mathbbm{i}\partial_{y}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{z}=\partial_{x}+\mathbbm{i}\partial_{y}
.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
Ada24
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

For each complex number
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
, there exists a unique collection
of correlation functions with the following properties:

(FER)

The correlation functions of the ground fermions are

\displaystyle\Big\langle\bm{\xi}(z_{1})\,\bm{\theta}(w_{1})\cdots\bm{\xi}(z_{n})\,\bm{\theta}(w_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,(4\pi)^{n}\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{\Omega}\big(z_{i},w_{\sigma(i)}\big)

for
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1}\ldots,w_{n})\in\textnormal{Conf}_{2n}(\Omega)
,
they vanish when the number of

\bm{\xi}
-insertions and
\bm{\theta}
-insertions do not coincide,
and they satisfy

\displaystyle\Big\langle\cdots\bm{\xi}(z)\,\bm{\theta}(w)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=-\,\Big\langle\cdots\bm{\theta}(w)\,\bm{\xi}(z)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,.

(DER)

For the Virasoro modes
{\mathsf{L}}_{-1}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{-1}
we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big[{\mathsf{L}}_{-1}\bm{\varphi}\big](z)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\ =\ \partial_{z}\,\Big\langle\bm{\varphi}(z)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

and

\displaystyle\;\Big\langle\big[\mspace{1mu}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.3pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{L}$}}}}\mathsf{L}_{-1}\bm{\varphi}\big](z)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\ =\ \mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{z}\,\Big\langle\bm{\varphi}(z)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,,

for any field
\bm{\varphi}\in\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
.

(CUR)

Within correlation functions, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}(z)\bm{\varphi}(w)\,=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\frac{\,\big[{\upchi}_{k}\bm{\varphi}\big](w)\,}{\,(z-w)^{k+1}}\,,\mspace{20mu}

\displaystyle\mspace{59mu}\overline{\bm{\chi}}(z)\bm{\varphi}(w)\,=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\frac{\,\big[\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{k}\bm{\varphi}\big](w)\,}{\,(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{k+1}}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}(z)\bm{\varphi}(w)\,=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\frac{\,\big[{\upeta}_{k}\bm{\varphi}\big](w)\,}{\,(z-w)^{k+1}}\,,\mspace{20mu}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mspace{20mu}\overline{\bm{\eta}}(z)\bm{\varphi}(w)\,=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\frac{\,\big[\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}\bm{\varphi}\big](w)\,}{\,(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{k+1}}

for any field
\bm{\varphi}\in\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
for small enough
|z-w|
.

(LOG)

We have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\bm{\xi}(z)\bm{\theta}(w)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,

\displaystyle\,\log\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\Big\langle\bm{\mathbbm{1}}(w)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,-\,\Big\langle\big[\bm{\omega}+\alpha\,\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big](w)\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

\displaystyle\,\,+\,o\big(|z-w|\big)\phantom{\Bigg|}\,

as
|z-w|\to 0
.

A proof of this theorem can be found in
[
Ada24
]
.
The following remarks are corollaries to this result and will be used later in the text.

Remark 2.4
.

Consider, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, the
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
elements

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}_{i}\,\coloneqq\,{\upchi}_{-k_{\alpha_{i}}^{(i)}}\cdots{\upchi}_{-k_{1}^{(i)}}{\upeta}_{-\ell_{\beta_{i}}^{(i)}}\cdots{\upeta}_{-\ell_{1}^{(i)}}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{\alpha}_{i}}^{(i)}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-\bar{k}_{1}^{(i)}}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{\beta}_{i}}^{(i)}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}^{(i)}}\bm{\omega}\in\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}\,,

in the basis (
2.1
).
By property
(CUR)
, their correlation functions can be obtained as

\displaystyle\Big\langle\bm{\varphi}_{1}(z_{1})\,\cdots\,\bm{\varphi}_{n}(z_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,\oint\!\cdots\!\oint\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg(\prod_{a=1}^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\textnormal{d}\zeta_{i;a}^{\chi}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\prod_{b=1}^{\beta_{i}}\frac{\textnormal{d}\zeta_{i;b}^{\eta}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\prod_{\bar{a}=1}^{\bar{\alpha}_{i}}\frac{\textnormal{d}\overline{\zeta}_{i;\bar{a}}^{\bar{\chi}}}{\overline{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}}\prod_{\bar{b}=1}^{\bar{\beta}_{i}}\frac{\textnormal{d}\overline{\zeta}_{i;\bar{b}}^{\bar{\eta}}}{\overline{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}}\Bigg)\times

\displaystyle\mspace{60mu}\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg(\prod_{a=1}^{\alpha_{i}}\,\big(\zeta_{i;a}^{\chi}-z_{i}\big)^{-k_{a}^{(i)}}\prod_{b=1}^{\beta_{i}}\,\big(\zeta_{i;b}^{\eta}-z_{i}\big)^{-\ell_{b}^{(i)}}\prod_{\bar{a}=1}^{\bar{\alpha}_{i}}\,\Big(\overline{\zeta_{i;\bar{a}}^{\bar{\chi}}-z_{i}}\Big)^{-\bar{k}_{\bar{a}}^{(i)}}\prod_{\bar{b}=1}^{\bar{\beta}_{i}}\,\Big(\overline{\zeta_{i;\bar{b}}^{\bar{\eta}}-z_{i}}\Big)^{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{b}}^{(i)}}\Bigg)\times

\displaystyle\times\!\bigg\langle\bm{\chi}\big(\zeta_{1;1}^{\chi}\big)\cdots\bm{\chi}\big(\zeta_{1;\alpha_{1}}^{\chi}\big)\bm{\eta}\big(\zeta_{1;1}^{\eta}\big)\cdots\bm{\eta}\big(\zeta_{1;\beta_{1}}^{\eta}\big)\overline{\bm{\chi}}\big(\zeta_{1;1}^{\bar{\chi}}\big)\cdots\overline{\bm{\chi}}\big(\zeta_{1;\bar{\alpha}_{1}}^{\bar{\chi}}\big)\overline{\bm{\eta}}\big(\zeta_{1;1}^{\bar{\eta}}\big)\cdots\overline{\bm{\eta}}\big(\zeta_{1;\bar{\beta}_{1}}^{\bar{\eta}}\big)\,\bm{\omega}(z_{1})\cdots\phantom{\Bigg|}

\displaystyle\mspace{10mu}\cdots\bm{\chi}\big(\zeta_{n;1}^{\chi}\big)\cdots\bm{\chi}\big(\zeta_{n;\alpha_{n}}^{\chi}\big)\bm{\eta}\big(\zeta_{n;1}^{\eta}\big)\cdots\bm{\eta}\big(\zeta_{n;\beta_{n}}^{\eta}\big)\overline{\bm{\chi}}\big(\zeta_{n;1}^{\bar{\chi}}\big)\cdots\overline{\bm{\chi}}\big(\zeta_{n;\bar{\alpha}_{n}}^{\bar{\chi}}\big)\overline{\bm{\eta}}\big(\zeta_{n;1}^{\bar{\eta}}\big)\cdots\overline{\bm{\eta}}\big(\zeta_{n;\bar{\beta}_{n}}^{\bar{\eta}}\big)\,\bm{\omega}(z_{n})\bigg\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

where the integrals are performed along non-intersecting contours in the following way:
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
, the variables
\zeta_{i;a}^{\chi}
,
\zeta_{i;b}^{\eta}
,
\zeta_{i;\bar{a}}^{\bar{\chi}}
and
\zeta_{i;\bar{b}}^{\bar{\eta}}
are integrated along nested contours around the point
z_{i}
in the radial order

\displaystyle\zeta_{i;1}^{\chi},\ldots,\zeta_{i;\alpha_{i}}^{\chi},\zeta_{i;1}^{\eta},\ldots,\zeta_{i;\beta_{i}}^{\eta},\zeta_{i;1}^{\bar{\chi}},\ldots,\zeta_{i;\bar{\alpha}_{i}}^{\bar{\chi}},\zeta_{i;1}^{\bar{\eta}},\ldots,\zeta_{i;\bar{\beta}_{i}}^{\bar{\eta}}

in the inward direction.

\diamond

Example 2.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\bm{\omega}(z)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,-\,4\pi\,\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)-\alpha\,,

where
\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}
is the harmonic part of the Green’s function
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega}
.
Note that this one-point function is related to the conformal radius
\mathrm{R}(z;\Omega)
of the domain
\Omega
from the point
z\in\Omega
since we have
\mathrm{R}(z;\Omega)=\exp\big(2\pi\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)\big)
.

\diamond

Remark 2.6
.

The correlation functions of the ground states
\bm{\xi}
,
\bm{\theta}
, and
\bm{\omega}
satisfy the following formula:
Take three non-intersecting collections
\underline{z}\coloneqq\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\}
,
\underline{w}\coloneqq\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
and
\underline{x}\coloneqq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
of distinct points in the domain
\Omega
.
Let
{\mathfrak{B}(\underline{z},\underline{w};\underline{x})}
denote the set of bijections from the set
{\underline{z}\cup\underline{x}}
onto the set
{\underline{w}\cup\underline{x}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\bm{\xi}(z_{1})\bm{\theta}(w_{1})

\displaystyle\cdots\bm{\xi}(z_{n})\bm{\theta}(w_{n})\bm{\omega}(x_{k})\cdots\bm{\omega}(x_{1})\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

\displaystyle=\phantom{\bigg|}(-1)^{k}\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\underline{z},\underline{w};\underline{x})}(-1)^{b}(-1)^{F_{b}}\prod_{\underset{b(y)\neq y}{y\in\underline{z}\cup\underline{x}}}\Big\langle\bm{\xi}(y)\bm{\theta}\big(b(y)\big)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\prod_{\underset{b(x)=x}{x\in\underline{x}}}\Big\langle\bm{\omega}(x)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,,

where
(-1)^{b}
is the signature of the bijection
b
, and
F_{b}
is the number of fixed points of
b
.

\diamond

2.2Tools of discrete complex analysis

Let us start by defining the lattices on which our discrete complex analysis tools take place; see Figure
2.1
.
For
\delta>0
, we define

•

the
primary lattice

\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,

•

the
dual lattice

\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{*}\coloneqq(\delta{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{\delta}{2})^{2}
, and

•

the
medial lattice

\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}\coloneqq\delta{\mathbb{Z}}\times(\delta{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{\delta}{2})\cup(\delta{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{\delta}{2})\times\delta{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Naturally, a medial vertex in
\delta{\mathbb{Z}}\times(\delta{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{\delta}{2})
we call a
vertical edge
, whereas a medial vertex in
(\delta{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{\delta}{2})\times\delta{\mathbb{Z}}
we call a
horizontal edge
.
Finally, we also define the
diamond lattice

\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\coloneqq\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\bullet}\cup\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{*}
.

\begin{overpic}[./figures/lattices.pdf]
\put(64.0,35.0){\large$\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\bullet}$ \hskip 3.0pt ({primary lattice)}}
\put(64.0,26.5){\large$\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{*}$ \hskip 3.0pt ({dual lattice)}}
\put(64.0,18.0){\large$\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}$ \hskip 3.0pt ({medial lattice)}}
\put(57.0,8.5){\large$\delta$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2.1:
Lattices involved in our tools of discrete complex analysis.

2.2.1.Discrete differential operators

We start by defining discrete versions of the Wirtinger derivatives.
Given a function
f\colon\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\to{\mathbb{C}}
on the diamond lattice, its
holomorphic
and
antiholomorphic discrete derivatives
are the functions
\partial_{\sharp}f,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}f\colon\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
on the medial lattice defined by the formulae

\displaystyle\phantom{\Bigg|}\partial_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z}):=\;

\displaystyle\frac{f(\mathbf{z}+\frac{\delta}{2})-f(\mathbf{z}-\frac{\delta}{2})}{2}-\mathbbm{i}\,\frac{f(\mathbf{z}+\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2})-f(\mathbf{z}-\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2})}{2}\,,\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\phantom{\Bigg|}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z}):=\;

\displaystyle\frac{f(\mathbf{z}+\frac{\delta}{2})-f(\mathbf{z}-\frac{\delta}{2})}{2}+\mathbbm{i}\,\frac{f(\mathbf{z}+\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2})-f(\mathbf{z}-\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2})}{2}\,.

Similarly, for a function
f
on the medial lattice, its holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivatives
\partial_{\sharp}f
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}f
are functions on the diamond lattice defined by the same formulae as above.

If a function
f
satisfies
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})=0
, it is said to be
discrete holomorphic
at
\mathbf{z}
.
Similarly, if it satisfies
\partial_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})=0
, then it is said to be
discrete antiholomorphic
at
\mathbf{z}
.

In our analysis, we also need versions of the holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivatives for functions defined only on the primary lattice.
For a function
f\colon\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\bullet}\to{\mathbb{C}}
on the primary lattice,
we define the functions
\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f\colon\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
by

\displaystyle\phantom{-}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})\,\coloneqq\,f\Big(\mathbf{z}+\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)-f\Big(\mathbf{z}-\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)\mspace{30mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{30mu}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})\,\coloneqq\,f\Big(\mathbf{z}+\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)-f\Big(\mathbf{z}-\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)

when
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
is a horizontal edge,
and by

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})\,\coloneqq\,-\mathbbm{i}\,\bigg(f\Big(\mathbf{z}+\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)-f\Big(\mathbf{z}-\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)\bigg)\mspace{20mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{20mu}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})\,\coloneqq\,\mathbbm{i}\,\bigg(f\Big(\mathbf{z}+\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)-f\Big(\mathbf{z}-\mathbbm{i}\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)\bigg)

when
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
is a vertical edge.

Lastly, we define the (combinatorially normalised)
discrete laplacian
operator
\Delta_{\sharp}
.
For a function
f
on any of the lattices, the function
\Delta_{\sharp}f
on the same lattice is defined by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z}):=\;

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{z}+\delta)+f(\mathbf{z}+\mathbbm{i}\delta)+f(\mathbf{z}-\delta)+f(\mathbf{z}-\mathbbm{i}\delta)-4\,f(\mathbf{z})\,.

Naturally, we say that a function
f
is
discrete harmonic
at
\mathbf{z}
if it satisfies
\Delta_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})=0
.

Remark 2.7
.

The discrete laplacian admits the following factorisations in terms of discrete differential operators:

\displaystyle\partial_{\sharp}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\partial_{\sharp}\,=\,\frac{1}{4}\,\Delta_{\sharp}\,,

and, for a function
f\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\to{\mathbb{C}}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\sharp}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})\,=\,\begin{cases}\mspace{10mu}\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{z})&\mspace{10mu}\textnormal{ if }\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\bullet}\,,\textnormal{ and }\\
&\\
\mspace{35mu}0&\mspace{10mu}\textnormal{ if }\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{*}\,.\end{cases}

We will use extensively these factorisations in our computations in later sections.

\diamond

2.2.2.Discrete integration

The second tool that we use in out treatment is the notion of discrete integration on the square lattice with unit mesh size —that is, when we set
\delta=1
— that was introduced in
[
HKV22
]
.
For that purpose, consider the
corner lattice

{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{c}}\coloneqq({\mathbb{Z}}\pm\frac{1}{4})^{2}
,
whose vertices we call
corners
.
A sequence
(c_{0},\ldots,c_{n})
of consecutively nearest corners is called a
corner path
.

Given two functions
f\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
g\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and a corner path
\gamma=(c_{0},\ldots,c_{n})
, we define

\displaystyle\int^{\sharp}_{\gamma}f(\mathbf{u}_{\diamond})g(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}\,(c_{j}-c_{j-1})f(\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\diamond})g(\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\textnormal{m}})\,,

and

\displaystyle\int^{\sharp}_{\gamma}f(\mathbf{u}_{\diamond})g(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}{\overline{\mathbf{u}}}\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}\,\overline{(c_{j}-c_{j-1})}f(\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\diamond})g(\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\textnormal{m}})\,,

where the diamond vertex
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\diamond}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}
and the medial vertex
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
are the unique ones that are closest to both
c_{j}
and
c_{j-1}
as shown in Figure
2.2
.
Note that these formulae are also well-defined when one of the integrands —either
f
or
g
— takes values in a complex vector space.

\begin{overpic}[./figures/contour_and_integration.pdf]
\put(78.8,14.0){$\mathbf{u}^{\textnormal{m}}_{j}$}
\put(93.0,14.0){$\mathbf{u}^{\diamond}_{j}$}
\put(87.7,18.3){$c_{j}$}
\put(87.7,5.4){$c_{j-1}$}
\put(-2.0,34.5){
\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}
\hbox{\pagecolor{white}\centering\Large\parbox{0.0pt}{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0} \hskip 0.0pt${\mathbb{Z}}^{2}$\@add@centering}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2.2:

A corner contour and, zoomed in,
an integration step
(c_{j-1},c_{j})

with its closest medial vertex
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
and diamond vertex
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\diamond}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}
.

A
corner contour
is a corner path
\gamma=(c_{0},\ldots,c_{n})
whose corners are all distinct except
c_{0}=c_{n}
.
The set of vertices in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
that are enclosed by a corner contour
\gamma
is called the
interior
of
\gamma
, and it is denoted by
\textnormal{int}\,\gamma
.
We also use the notations
{\textnormal{int}_{\diamond}\gamma\coloneqq\textnormal{int}\,\gamma\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}}
and

{\textnormal{int}_{\textnormal{m}}\gamma\coloneqq\textnormal{int}\,\gamma\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}}
.
A corner contour is said to be
positively oriented
if it encircles its interior in a counterclockwise fashion.
We use the notation
\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}
for discrete integration along a positively-oriented corner contour
\gamma
.

The following property of this notion of discrete integration are the ones that make it suitable for our analysis and will appear throughout the present text in our computations.
For a positively-oriented corner contour
\gamma
,
and any two functions
f\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
g\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we have the
discrete Stokes’ formulae

\displaystyle\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}f(\mathbf{u}_{\diamond})g(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}{\mathbf{u}}=\;\phantom{-}\,\mathbbm{i}\,\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{\diamond}\in\textnormal{int}_{\diamond}\gamma}f(\mathbf{v}_{\diamond})\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}g(\mathbf{v}_{\diamond})\,+\,\mathbbm{i}\,\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}}\in\textnormal{int}_{\textnormal{m}}\gamma}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\,g(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\,,

\displaystyle\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}f(\mathbf{u}_{\diamond})g(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}{\overline{\mathbf{u}}}=\;-\,\mathbbm{i}\,\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{\diamond}\in\textnormal{int}_{\diamond}\gamma}f(\mathbf{v}_{\diamond})\,\partial_{\sharp}g(\mathbf{v}_{\diamond})\,-\,\mathbbm{i}\,\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}}\in\textnormal{int}_{\textnormal{m}}\gamma}\partial_{\sharp}f(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\,g(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\,.

Note that, from the discrete Stokes’ formula, it follows that one can deform the corner contour of integration without changing the value of the integral as long as the integrands are discrete (anti)holomorphic in the appropriate domain.

2.2.3.Discrete complex monomials

The last piece we need in our analysis are the discrete analogues of the complex monomials
z\mapsto z^{n}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
introduced in
[
HKV22
]
with a small modification—see
[
Ada25
,
ABK24
]
for the details and discussion of this modification.

Proposition 2.8

(
[
HKV22
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

There exists a unique family of
{\mathbb{C}}
-valued functions
\{\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{u}^{[n]}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
on

{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
that satisfies the following properties:

1.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the function
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{u}^{[n]}
has the same square-grid symmetries
as the Laurent monomial
z\mapsto z^{n}
,
i.e.,
(\mathbbm{i}\mathbf{u})^{[n]}=\mathbbm{i}^{n}\mathbf{u}^{[n]}
and
\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{[n]}=\overline{\mathbf{u}^{[n]}}
for all

\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
.

2.

For all
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
,
\mathbf{u}^{[0]}=1
and, for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\partial_{\sharp}\mathbf{u}^{[n]}=n\,\mathbf{u}^{[n-1]}
.

3.

For each
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
, there exists an
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathbf{u}^{[n]}=0
for all
n\geq N
.

4.

For
n<0
, we have
\mathbf{u}^{[n]}\to 0
as
|\mathbf{u}|\rightarrow\infty
.

5.

The first negative-power monomial has the following explicit
failure of discrete holomorphicity near the origin

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\mathbf{u}^{[-1]}=\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{\mathbf{u},0}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{|\mathbf{v}|=\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}+\frac{1}{8}\sum_{|\mathbf{v}|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\mspace{-5mu}\delta_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}\,.

6.

For any
n\geq 0
and all
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
we have
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\mathbf{u}^{[n]}=0
.
For any
n<0
, we have
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\mathbf{u}^{[n]}=0
except at finitely many
points
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\diamond}\cup{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
.

7.

For any
n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have the discrete residue formula

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\mathbf{u}^{[n]}_{\diamond}\,\mathbf{u}^{[m]}_{\textnormal{m}}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\;=\;\delta_{n+m+1}\,,

for any large enough positively-oriented corner contour
\gamma
that encircles the origin.

8.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, as
{|\mathbf{u}|\to\infty}
, the discrete monomial has the asymptotics

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[n]}=\mathbf{u}^{n}+o(|\mathbf{u}|^{n})\,,

where in
\mathbf{u}^{n}
we interpret
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{C}}
via the embedding
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.

3Local fields of the fDGFF

In this section, we introduce the discrete framework underlying our results.
More precisely, we construct a space of local fields for the fermionic discrete Gaussian free field —see also
[
Abd04
,
CCRR23
]
— which we subsequently show to carry a representation of the symplectic fermions algebra in a meaningful way.

We start in Section 3.1 by defining the fermionic discrete Gaussian free field in general discrete domains of
\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
Then, in Section 3.2, we give the definition of local fields of the model.
Finally, in Section 3.3 we define certain operators on the space of local fields —the current modes— that enable the algebraic analysis of this space.

3.1The fermionic discrete Gaussian free field

In this section we define the discrete model of interest, the
fermionic discrete Gaussian free field
(fDGFF).
For simplicity, we consider our model in discrete domains whose boundary is a polygonal Jordan curve
\gamma
on
{\mathbb{C}}
consisting of vertical and horizontal segments and with corners on
\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.
Then, we define a
discrete
(
Jordan
)
domain
as
\Omega^{\delta}\coloneqq\overline{\textnormal{int}\,{\gamma}}\cap\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and its boundary as
\partial\Omega^{\delta}\coloneqq\gamma\cap\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\subset\Omega^{\delta}
.
We will consider discrete domains
\Omega^{\delta}
whose interior
\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega^{\delta}
, denoted by
\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}
, is path-connected via nearest-neighbour paths.

Given a set
X
, the
Grassmann algebra generated by

X
is the exterior algebra of the vector space
\bigoplus_{x\in X}{\mathbb{C}}\,x
.
We use the notation
x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}
for the product of
n
algebra elements —typically denoted as
x_{1}\wedge x_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge x_{n}
in the literature of exterior algebras—.
Recall that the product in the Grassmann algebra is anticommutative, which in our notation reads as
x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}x_{1}=0
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
.

Let
\Omega^{\delta}\subset\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
be a discrete domain.
Let
\textnormal{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]
denote Grassmann algebra generated by the set
\{\xi(\mathbf{z}),\theta(\mathbf{z})\colon\mathbf{z}\in\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}\}
; this space constitutes the observables of the fDGFF on
\Omega^{\delta}
.
Then, the (normalized) correlation functions of the fDGFF on
\Omega^{\delta}
(with Dirichlet boundary conditions) are linear maps of the type

\displaystyle\big\langle\cdot\big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\;\colon\;\text{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]\;\longrightarrow\;{\mathbb{C}}\,.

In Remark
3.1
below, we give explicit formulae for all the corelation functions of the fDGFF on any discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
.
However, the correlation functions are typically defined via the Berezin notion of integration.
To define it, we take a total order on the set
\Omega^{\delta}
and consider the vector-space basis

\displaystyle\bigg\{\prod_{\mathbf{z}\in S_{1}}^{\rightarrow}\xi(\mathbf{z})\prod_{\mathbf{w}\in S_{2}}^{\rightarrow}\theta(\mathbf{w})\bigg\}_{S_{1},S_{2}\subset\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}

where the arrow (
\rightarrow
) indicates that the order-dependent product is taken increasingly from left to right in the total order of
\Omega^{\delta}
.
Then, the
Berezin integral
over the Grassmann algebra
\textnormal{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]
is the linear map

\displaystyle\iint\cdot\;\textnormal{d}\xi\textnormal{d}\theta\;\colon\;\textnormal{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}

that satisfies

\displaystyle\iint\Bigg(\prod_{\mathbf{z}\in\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}\!\!\xi(\mathbf{z})\theta(\mathbf{z})\Bigg)\textnormal{d}\xi\textnormal{d}\theta\,=\,1\,,

and takes the value
0
on the all basis elements except the one indexed by
S_{1}=S_{2}=\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}
—since it is proportional to
\prod_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}}\xi(\mathbf{z})\theta(\mathbf{z})
—.

Then, to define the correlation functions of the fDGFF on
\Omega^{\delta}
, we consider the quadratic action

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\xi,\theta\big]\;\coloneqq\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\!\!\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}\!\!\xi(\mathbf{z})\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}}\theta(\mathbf{z})

where
\theta
and
\xi
are conceived as maps
\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]
,
and
\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}}
denotes the Laplacian operator with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Omega^{\delta}
, that is,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}}\theta(\mathbf{z})\,\coloneqq\!\sum_{\underset{|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{w}|=\delta}{\mathbf{w}\in\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}}\!\!\!\theta(\mathbf{w})\,-\,4\,\theta(\mathbf{z})\,.

Then, for any element
F\in\textnormal{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]
we define its
fDGFF correlation function
as

\displaystyle\big\langle F\;\!\big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\;\coloneqq\;\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega^{\delta}}}\int\!\!\!\int F\;e^{-\mathbf{S}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]}\textnormal{d}\xi\textnormal{d}\theta\,,

where
\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\coloneqq\iint e^{-\mathbf{S}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]}\textnormal{d}\xi\textnormal{d}\theta
.

For proofs of the statements in the following remark, we refer the reader to
[
Abd04
]
.

Remark 3.1
.

The correlation function of the monomial
\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\theta(\mathbf{w}_{m})
vanishes unless
n=m
.
For the two-point function, we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z})\theta(\mathbf{w})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\;=\;4\pi\,\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})\,,

where
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
is —up to a negative sign— the inverse of the Dirichlet Laplacian, that is, it satisfies

\displaystyle\big(\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}}\big)_{(1)}\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})\,=\,-\delta_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}}

for
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}\in\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}
, where the subindex
(\,\cdot\,)_{(1)}
indicates the Laplacian operator is taken with respect to the first variable of
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
.
Finally, the
2n
-point functions satisfy the
fermionic Wick’s formula
, i.e. we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\;=\;\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}_{i})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{\sigma(i)})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}

where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
denotes the symmetric group of order
n
and
(-1)^{\sigma}
is the signature of the permutation
\sigma
.

\diamond

We extend the correlation functions of the fDGFF to the boundary
\partial\Omega^{\delta}
by declaring
\big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{n})\big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}=0
whenever at least one of the insertion points
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n}
sits on
\partial\Omega^{\delta}
.
Note this does not spoil the discrete harmonicity of the correlation functions near the boundary since
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
is the Dirichlet Green’s function.

The following remark will be useful when we discuss scaling limits in Section
4.2

Remark 3.2
.

In a similar fashion as in Remark
2.6
, for three non-intersecting collections

\underline{\mathbf{z}}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n}\}
,

\underline{\mathbf{w}}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n}\}
,
and
\underline{\mathbf{x}}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}\}

of points in the discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
,
the set of bijections from the set
{\underline{\mathbf{z}}\cup\underline{\mathbf{x}}}
onto the set
{\underline{\mathbf{w}}\cup\underline{\mathbf{x}}}
is denoted by
{\mathfrak{B}(\underline{\mathbf{z}},\underline{\mathbf{w}};\underline{\mathbf{x}})}
.
For any fixed constant
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
, a straightforward manipulation of the fermionic Wick formula —Remark
3.1
— leads to

\displaystyle\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{n})\,\big(\,\xi(\mathbf{x}_{1})\,\theta(\mathbf{x}_{1})+c\,\big)\cdots\big(\,\xi(\mathbf{x}_{k})\,\theta(\mathbf{x}_{k})+c\,\big)\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\phantom{\Bigg|}

\displaystyle\mspace{40mu}\phantom{\Bigg|}=(-1)^{k}\!\!\sum_{b\in\mathfrak{B}(\underline{\mathbf{z}},\underline{\mathbf{w}};\underline{\mathbf{x}})}\!\!\!(-1)^{b}(-1)^{F_{b}}\prod_{\underset{b(y)\neq y}{y\in\underline{\mathbf{z}}\cup\underline{\mathbf{x}}}}\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{y})\,\theta\big(b(\mathbf{y})\big)\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\prod_{\underset{b(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{x}\in\underline{\mathbf{x}}}}\bigg(\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{x})\,\theta(\mathbf{x})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}+\,c\,\bigg),

where
(-1)^{b}
is the signature of the bijection
b
, and
F_{b}
is the number of fixed points of
b
.

\diamond

3.2Local fields of the fDGFF

We now define the local observables in the discrete setting that we aim to analise and put in one-to-one correspondence with the local fields of a CFT.

3.2.1.Field polynomials

The space of
field polynomials
1
1

1

Note that, actually, they are
not
polynomials since they are built with anticommuting variables.
of the fDGFF is

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\;\coloneqq\;\textnormal{Gr}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}[\bm{\upxi},\bm{\uptheta}]\,,

that is, a field polynomial is an element of the Grassmann algebra over the set of generators
\{\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}),\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\,\colon\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\}
.
When necessary, we will stress the product in
\mathcal{P}
with a dot (
\,\cdot\,
).
The
support
of a field polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}
, denoted by
\textnormal{supp}\,P
is the smallest subset
S\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
such that we have

\displaystyle P\,\in\,\textnormal{Gr}_{S}[\bm{\upxi},\bm{\uptheta}]\,\subset\,\textnormal{Gr}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}[\bm{\upxi},\bm{\uptheta}]\,=\,\mathcal{P}\,.

The way to obtain an fDGFF observable is via the
evaluation map
:
given a discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
and point
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}
therein, we define the map

\displaystyle\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}}\;:\;\mathcal{P}\;\longrightarrow\;\textnormal{Gr}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\xi,\theta]\,,

as the unique associative algebra homomorphism that on the generators acts as

\displaystyle\text{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\bf{z}}\big(\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u})\big)\,=\,\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})\mspace{40mu}\text{ and }\mspace{40mu}\text{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\bf{z}}\big(\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\big)\,=\,\theta(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})

where
\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})
and
\theta(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})
are interpreted as
0
if
\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u}
sits outside of
{\Omega^{\delta}}
.

Example 3.3
.

Given a medial point
\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}
, the field polynomials

\displaystyle\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,\coloneqq\,\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,,

\displaystyle\mspace{40mu}\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,\coloneqq\,\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,,\mspace{50mu}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip-0.7pt\overline{\scalebox{1.1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upchi}$}}}}\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,\coloneqq\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,,

\displaystyle\mspace{40mu}\phantom{\Big|}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,\coloneqq\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})

are called, respectively, the
holomorphic
and
antiholomorphic currrents
.

\diamond

3.2.2.Null field polynomials

A field polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}
is said to be
null

if, for any discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
and any insertion point
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\,\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}}(P)\,\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\theta(\mathbf{w}_{m})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,=\,0

whenever the distances
|\,\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}_{i}|
,
|\,\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{w}_{i}|
, and
\textnormal{d}(\mathbf{z},\partial\Omega^{\delta})
are large enough.
We let
\mathcal{N}
denote the subspace of null field polynomials.
Note that, while
\mathcal{N}
is a vector subspace, it is
not
an ideal; furthermore, note the product of two null field polynomials is not necessarily null.

In the following, we provide some examples of field polynomials which are null and not null.
Let us start with a field polynomial that is
not
null.

Example 3.4
.

The field polynomial
1\in\mathcal{P}
is not null since we have, for example,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\,\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}}(1)\,\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,=\,\Big\langle\,\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,=\,4\pi\,\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}(\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{1})\,\neq\,0

for any discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
and any points
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}
and
\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{1}\in\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}
.

\diamond

Let us now see examples of field polynomials that
are
null.

Example 3.5
.

For any
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, the field polynomials

\displaystyle\phantom{\bigg|}\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u})\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{\underset{|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}|=1}{\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}\!\!\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{v})-4\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u})\mspace{50mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{50mu}\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{\underset{|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}|=1}{\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}\!\!\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{v})-4\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\phantom{\bigg|}

are null.
This can be straightforwardly verified from Remark
3.1
.
Moreover, using Wick’s formula, one can see that, if
P\in\mathcal{P}
is a field polynomial satisfying
\mathbf{u}\notin\textnormal{supp}\,P
, the field polynomials
\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u})\cdot P
and
\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\cdot P
are null, too.

\diamond

Example 3.6
.

By virtue of the factorisation of the Laplacian —Remark
2.7
— and Example
3.5
, we have that the field polynomials

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u})\,,\mspace{50mu}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{u})\,,\mspace{50mu}\partial_{\sharp}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.7pt\overline{\scalebox{1.1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upchi}$}}}}\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u})\,,\mspace{40mu}\textnormal{and}\mspace{40mu}\partial_{\sharp}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{u})

are null for any
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

\diamond

Example 3.7
.

The field polynomial
\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u})\bm{\uptheta}(0)\bm{\upxi}(0)+4\pi\delta_{0,\mathbf{u}}\bm{\upxi}(0)
is null.
To see this, we evaluate it at some
\mathbf{z}
in some discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
and test it against distant insertions.
Using Wick’s formula and the multilinearity of correlation functions, we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Big(\Delta_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\,\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})\theta(\mathbf{z})+4\pi\delta_{0,\mathbf{u}}\Big)\,\xi(\mathbf{z})\cdot\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\theta(\mathbf{z}_{m})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\phantom{\bigg|}

\displaystyle\,=\bigg(\big(\Delta_{\sharp}\big)_{\mathbf{u}}\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})\theta(\mathbf{z})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}+4\pi\delta_{0,\mathbf{u}}\,\bigg)\,\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z})\,\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\theta(\mathbf{w}_{m})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}

\displaystyle\mspace{20mu}-\,\big(\Delta_{\sharp}\big)_{\mathbf{u}}\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})\xi(\mathbf{z})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,\Big\langle\theta(\mathbf{z})\,\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\theta(\mathbf{w}_{m})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\phantom{\Bigg|}

\displaystyle\mspace{20mu}-\,\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\big(\Delta_{\sharp}\big)_{\mathbf{u}}\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})\xi(\mathbf{z}_{i})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z})\,\theta(\mathbf{z})\cdots\widehat{\xi(\mathbf{w}_{i})}\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\phantom{\Bigg|}

\displaystyle\mspace{20mu}-\,\sum_{j}(-1)^{n+j}\big(\Delta_{\sharp}\big)_{\mathbf{u}}\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z}+\delta\mathbf{u})\theta(\mathbf{w}_{j})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,\Big\langle\xi(\mathbf{z})\,\theta(\mathbf{z})\cdots\widehat{\theta(\mathbf{w}_{j})}\cdots\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\phantom{\Bigg|}\,,

where the hat (
\,\widehat{\phantom{m}}\;\!
) indicates that a term does not appear in the product.
Note that in the right-hand side of the above equation, the first factor in each term vanishes, proving the nullity of the given field polynomial.

\diamond

3.2.3.Local fields

The space of local observables that is actually meaningful is the quotient

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,\coloneqq\,\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{N}\,.

The elements of
\mathcal{F}
we refer to as
local fields
of the fDGFF.
We use the notation
P+\textnormal{\small Null}
for the equivalence class of
P\in\mathcal{P}
in the quotient
\mathcal{F}
.

Example 3.8
.

In Theorem
4.1
below, we prove that the space of local fields
\mathcal{F}
is isomorphic to the logarithmic Fock space of the symplectic fermions.
Through that isomorphism the local fields

\displaystyle\mspace{76mu}\mathbf{1}\,\coloneqq\,1+\textnormal{\small Null}\mspace{50mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{50mu}\bm{\upomega}\,\coloneqq-\bm{\upxi}(0)\bm{\uptheta}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}

correspond to the identity field and its logarithmic partner, respectively,
and the local fields

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\upxi}\,\coloneqq\,\bm{\upxi}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}\mspace{50mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{50mu}\bm{\uptheta}\,\coloneqq\bm{\uptheta}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}

correspond to the fermionic ground states.

\diamond

3.3Current modes

Our goal is to understand the structure of the space
\mathcal{F}
in relation to the scaling limit of (the evaluation of representatives of) its elements within correlation functions.
We take CFT as a source of inspiration for that.
In the symplectic fermions CFT, the currents —that is the fields
\bm{\chi}
,
\bm{\eta}
,
\overline{\bm{\chi}}
, and
\overline{\bm{\eta}}
— are fields that, in a sense, carry the symmetry algebra as their Fourier modes.
More precisely, inside correlation functions and for
|z-w|
small enough, we have the convergent series

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}(z)\bm{\varphi}(w)\,=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\frac{\big[\bm{\chi}_{k}\bm{\varphi}\big](w)}{(z-w)^{k+1}}\,,

and similar formulae for the other currents—see Property
(CUR)
in Theorem
2.3
.
Series expansions of this type are called
operator product expansions
(OPEs) and their existence is a fundamental pillar of two-dimensional conformal field theory.

In the case of the symplectic fermions, due to the holomorphicity of
\bm{\chi}
within correlation functions, that means that, in the continuum theory, we can obtain the correlation functions of the field
[\bm{\chi}_{k}\bm{\varphi}]
as by contour integrating
\bm{\chi}
around
\bm{\varphi}
with the appropriate coefficient.
Although such converging expansion are not avilable in the discrete, we can define operators that mimic the extraction of Laurent coefficients with the tools of discrete complex analysis that we described in Section
2.2
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
P+\textnormal{\small Null}\in\mathcal{F}
be a local field.
The linear operators

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}\big(P+\textnormal{\small Null}\big)

\displaystyle\,\coloneqq\,\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\frac{\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\,\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}_{k}\big(P+\textnormal{\small Null}\big)

\displaystyle\,\coloneqq\,\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\frac{\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\,\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{k}\big(P+\textnormal{\small Null}\big)

\displaystyle\,\coloneqq\,-\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\frac{\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{u}}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\,\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.7pt\overline{\scalebox{1.1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upchi}$}}}}\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,,\mspace{20mu}\text{ and }

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k}\big(P+\textnormal{\small Null}\big)

\displaystyle\,\coloneqq\,-\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\frac{\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{u}}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\,\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,,

(3.4)

where
\gamma
is a large enough discrete corner contour, are well-defined.

Proof.

The operators are well-defined if they do not depend on the choice of corner contour
\gamma
and the choice of field-polynomial representative
P
.
A straightforward application of the discrete Stokes’ formula —see Section
2.2.2
— combined with the discrete holomorphicity of the discrete monomials —Proposition
2.8
— and the null fields in Example
3.5
is enough to see that the operators are well-defined.
To see that the operator does not depend on the representative
P
, one just needs to remember that the product of two field polynomials with supports that are at a large enough distance is null if one of the two field polynomials is null.
For a more detailed proof see Lemmata 4.2 and 4.3 in
[
Ada25
]
.
∎

We refer to the operators (
3.1
)-(
3.2
) and (
3.3
)-(
3.4
) as the
holomorphic and antiholomorphic

current modes
, respectively.
They constitute a representation of two anticommuting copies of the symplectic fermions algebra, as we state in the following proposition.

Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
denote the usual anticommutator of operators, that is
\{A,B\}\coloneqq AB+BA
.

Proposition 3.10
.

For all
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
the current modes satisfy

\displaystyle\{\bm{\eta}_{k},\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,=\,k\,\bm{\delta}_{k+\ell}\,\textnormal{id}_{\mathcal{F}}\,=\,\{\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,,

\displaystyle\{\bm{\eta}_{k},\bm{\eta}_{\ell}\}\,=\,\{\bm{\chi}_{k},\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,=\,\{\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{\ell}\}\,=\,\{\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,=\,0\,,

and

\displaystyle\{\bm{\eta}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{\ell}\}\,=\,\{\bm{\chi}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,=\,\{\bm{\eta}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,=\,\{\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k},\bm{\chi}_{\ell}\}\,=\,0\,.

Proof.

The proof that each sector —holomorphic and antiholomorphic— constitutes a representation of the symplectic fermions algebra follows the same lines as the proof of Proposition 4.4 in
[
Ada25
]
.
For the anticommutativity of the sectors with one another, we write:

\displaystyle\big[\{\bm{\chi}_{k},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{\ell}\}\big]\big(P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,\big)=\;

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathbbm{i})^{2}}\bigg[\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma_{+}}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\,\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})

\displaystyle\mspace{120mu}+\;\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma_{-}}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\,\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\bigg]\cdot P\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathbbm{i})^{2}}\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\bigg[\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma_{+}}\,-\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma_{-}}\bigg]\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\,\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathbbm{i}}{(2\pi\mathbbm{i})^{2}}\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\sum_{\underset{\mathbf{u}_{\diamond}\notin\textnormal{int}_{\diamond}\gamma_{-}}{\mathbf{u}_{\diamond}\in\textnormal{int}_{\diamond}\gamma_{+}}}\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\diamond}\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\diamond})\mathrlap{\hskip-0.2pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\upeta}$}}}}\bm{\upeta}(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathbbm{i}}{2(2\pi\mathbbm{i})^{2}}\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\sum_{\underset{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}\notin\textnormal{int}_{\bullet}\gamma_{-}}{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}\in\textnormal{int}_{\bullet}\gamma_{+}}}\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\bullet}\,\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}_{\bullet})\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot P\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\,-\frac{4\pi\mathbbm{i}}{2(2\pi\mathbbm{i})^{2}}\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\sum_{\underset{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}\notin\textnormal{int}_{\bullet}\gamma_{-}}{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}\in\textnormal{int}_{\bullet}\gamma_{+}}}\mathbf{u}^{[k]}_{\bullet}\,\big(\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\big)_{\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}}}\big(\delta_{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet},\mathbf{v}_{\textnormal{m}}}\big)\cdot P\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\,-\frac{4\pi\mathbbm{i}}{2(2\pi\mathbbm{i})^{2}}\bigg[\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{v}}\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{[\ell]}_{\diamond}\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\mathbf{v}^{[k]}_{\textnormal{m}}\bigg]\,P\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,.

The discrete integral in square brackets vanishes for all values of
k
and
\ell
; see the proof of Proposition 4.2 in
[
ABK24
]
.
∎

In other words, the current modes render the space of local fields of the fDGFF a representation of the symmetry algebra of the symplectic fermions CFT.

Corollary 3.11
.

The map
\textnormal{{SF}}\oplus\overline{\textnormal{{SF}}}\to\textnormal{End}(\mathcal{F})
given by (the linear extension of)

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{\upeta}_{k}\,\longmapsto\,\bm{\eta}_{k}\,,\mspace{90mu}{\upchi}_{k}\,\longmapsto\,\bm{\chi}_{k}\,,\mspace{90mu}\mathsf{k}\,\longmapsto\,\textnormal{id}_{\mathcal{F}}\,,
