\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{k}\,\longmapsto\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k}\,,\mspace{90mu}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{k}\,\longmapsto\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{k}\,,\mspace{20mu}\text{ and }\mspace{30mu}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\mathsf{k}$}}}}\mathsf{k}\,\longmapsto\,\textnormal{id}_{\mathcal{F}}

is a Lie superalgebra homomorphism.

We now present some remarks and examples that will be useful to prove the isomorphism between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
.

Remark 3.12
.

The current modes of index
0
satisfy

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{0}\mspace{30mu}\text{ and }\mspace{30mu}\bm{\eta}_{0}\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{0}

as linear operators
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
.
This can be checked by a straightforward application of the discrete Stokes’ formula —Section
2.2.2
—.

\diamond

Remark 3.13
.

The ground states satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,-\bm{\upxi}\,,\mspace{150mu}\bm{\eta}_{0}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\bm{\uptheta}\,,\phantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}\bm{\uptheta}\,=\,\mathbf{1}\,,\mspace{65mu}\text{ and }\mspace{66mu}\bm{\eta}_{0}\bm{\upxi}\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\mathbf{1}\,.\phantom{\Big|}

Let us explicitly write down the computation for the first of these relations.
We have, for a sufficiently large corner contour
\gamma
encircling the origin,

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,

\displaystyle\,\sqint^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\frac{\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}}{2\pi\mathbbm{i}}\,\mathbf{u}^{[0]}_{\diamond}\,\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\cdot\Big(-\bm{\upxi}(0)\bm{\uptheta}(0)\Big)+\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\diamond}\in\textnormal{int}_{\diamond}\gamma}\partial_{\sharp}\bm{\upchi}(\mathbf{u}_{\diamond})\bm{\upxi}(0)\bm{\uptheta}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}\in\textnormal{int}_{\bullet}\gamma}\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}_{\bullet})\bm{\upxi}(0)\bm{\uptheta}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}\in\textnormal{int}_{\bullet}\gamma}4\pi\delta_{0,\mathbf{u}_{\bullet}}\bm{\upxi}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\bm{\upxi}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}\phantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\,-\bm{\upxi}\,,\phantom{\Big|}

where we have used the discrete Stokes’ formula —Section
2.2.2
—, the factorisation of the discrete Laplacian operator —Remark
2.7
—, and the null field from Example
3.7
.

\diamond

Example 3.14
.

With a simple application of the discrete Stokes’ formula and the discrete monomials value
0^{[k]}=0
for
k>0
—Section
2.2.3
—, one can check that all four ground states (
\mathbf{1}
,
\bm{\upxi}
,
\bm{\uptheta}
, and
\bm{\upomega}
) are annihilated by the current modes with positive index.
That is, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{k}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,\bm{\eta}_{k}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k}\bm{\upomega}\,=\,0\,,

and similarly for
\mathbf{1}
,
\bm{\upxi}
, and
\bm{\uptheta}
.

\diamond

3.3.1.The Sugawara construction

In Section
2.1.1
, we discussed how the logarithmic Fock space of the sympletic fermions becomes a representation of the Virasoro algebra via the so-called Sugawara construction.
In the case of the space
\mathcal{F}
, the following property enables this construction to work.

Lemma 3.15
.

For any local field
P+\textnormal{\small Null}
, there exists
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
such that for all
n\geq N
we have

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{n}\big(\,P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,\big)\,=\,\bm{\eta}_{n}\big(\,P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,\big)\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{n}\big(\,P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,\big)\,=\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{n}\big(\,P+\textnormal{\small Null}\,\big)\,=\,0\,.

Proof.

Fix a corner contour
\gamma
for the action —(
3.1
)–(
3.4
)— of any current mode on
P+\textnormal{\small Null}
.
Since the discrete neighbourhood of the origin where the discrete monomials
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{u}^{[k]}
vanishes grows with
k
, we just need to pick
N
big enough such that
\mathbf{u}^{[N]}
vanishes (in the interior of and) around the contour
\gamma
.
Then, by formulae (
3.1
)–(
3.4
), it is clear that the action of
\bm{\chi}_{n}
,
\bm{\eta}_{n}
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{n}
, and
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{n}
on
P+\textnormal{\small Null}
vanishes for
n\geq N
.
∎

Formally, the arguments in the proof of Lemma
2.2
prove the following statement too.

Lemma 3.16
.

The linear operators
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
given by the formal sums

\displaystyle{\mathbf{L}}_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k\,\geq\,n/2}\bm{\chi}_{n-k}\bm{\eta}_{k}-\sum_{k\,<\,n/2}\bm{\eta}_{k}\bm{\chi}_{n-k}\mspace{30mu}\text{ and }\mspace{30mu}\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k\,\geq\,n/2}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{n-k}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k}-\sum_{k\,<\,n/2}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{k}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{n-k}\,,

for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, are well-defined and constitute two commuting representations of the Virasoro algebra with central charge
c=-2
.

See Lemma
2.2
for the precise formulae of the commutators.

4Results

We now proceed to state and discuss the main results of this paper—their proofs are postponed for Section
5
.

In Section
4.1
, we present our first main result —Theorem
4.1
—, which concerns the local fields of the fDGFF.
In particular, the space
\mathcal{F}
of fDGFF local fields is in one-to-one correspondence with the logarithmic Fock space of the symplectic fermions.
This correspondence becomes meaningful only thanks to our second main result —Theorem
4.2
— regarding the scaling limit of correlation functions, which is presented in Section
4.2
.
Informally, the scaling limit of the —appropriately renormalised— correlation functions of fDGFF local fields are the correlation functions of the corresponding —via the correspondence in Theorem
4.1
— symplectic fermions local fields.
We end the section by discussing some applications of these results to two models of statistical mechanics—the uniform spanning tree and the Abelian sandpile model.
Certain local observables in those models can be computed with the correlation functions of the fermionic DGFF—we discuss how to interpret the scaling limit of such observables as local fields in the symplectic fermions CFT in Section
4.3
.

4.1Local fields of the fDGFF

The one-to-one correspondence between the spaces of local observables in the discrete and the continuum is phrased algebraically—we prove those two spaces to be isomorphic as representations of the symplectic fermions symmetry algebra.
This approach is not only a tool but a feature that provides information about the discrete local fields at hand.
In particular, the appropriate way of renormalising the correlation functions in the discrete so as to recover non-trivial scaling limits is dictated by the algebraic structure of the space of fields.
This will become clear in Theorem
4.2
below.

Theorem 4.1
.

As representations of the symplectic fermions algebra, we have the isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,\cong\,\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}\,.

The proof is presented in Sections
5.1
,
5.2
and
5.3
.

One immediate consequence of this theorem is that,
in a similar fashion as the logarithmic Fock space —Section
2.1.1
—,
the space
\mathcal{F}
admits the basis

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{-k_{r}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{s}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}}\bm{\upomega}

(4.1)

with

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}r,s,\bar{r},\bar{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

and the orderings

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0\leq k_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots<k_{r}}\,,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\cdots<\ell_{s}}\,,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0<\bar{k}_{1}<\bar{k}_{2}<\cdots<\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\,,\text{ and }

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}{0<\bar{\ell}_{1}<\bar{\ell}_{2}<\cdots<\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\,.

Before moving on, we comment on the Virasoro structure of
\mathcal{F}
, which plays a key role in the scaling limit of correlation functions.

The operators
{\mathbf{L}}_{0}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0}
—defined in Lemma
2.2
— provide a natural
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
-bigrading
of the space
\mathcal{F}
via their generalised eigenspaces.
The generalised eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{L}}_{0}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0}
are called, respectively,
holomorphic
and
antiholomorphic generalised conformal dimensions
.
The bigrading can be expressed simply in terms of the basis (
4.1
):
a straightforward computation leads to

\displaystyle\Big[\,{\mathbf{L}}_{0}-\Delta_{\mathbf{k},\bm{\ell}}\,\Big]^{2}\big(\bm{\chi}_{-k_{r}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{s}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}}\bm{\upomega}\big)\,=\,0\,,

and

\displaystyle\Big[\,\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0}-\bar{\Delta}_{\bar{\mathbf{k}},\bar{\bm{\ell}}}\,\Big]^{2}\big(\bm{\chi}_{-k_{r}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{s}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}}\bm{\upomega}\big)\,=\,0\,,

where
\Delta_{\bf{k},\bf{l}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{r}k_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{s}\ell_{j}

and

\bar{\Delta}_{\bar{\bf{k}},\bar{\bf{l}}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{\bar{r}}\bar{k}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{s}}\bar{\ell}_{j}
are the generalised conformal dimensions of the basis state.
In particular, this implies that the Virasoro modes
{\mathbf{L}}_{0}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0}
have Jordan blocks of rank at most
2
.
The generalised
({\mathbf{L}}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0})
-eigenvalue of a field is referred to as its
generalised scaling dimension
.

4.2Scaling limit of correlation functions

For the rest of this section, we let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}
be a (open, simply-connected, proper, non-empty) domain of the complex plane,
and we take distinct points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}\in\Omega
thereon.
We also let
\Omega^{\delta}\subset\delta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
be a family of discrete domains such that
\Omega^{\delta}\to\Omega
as
\delta\downarrow 0
in the Carathéodory sense
2
2

2

More precisely, what converges in the Carathéodory sense are the Jordan domains bounded by
\partial\Omega^{\delta}
.
.
Finally, we let
\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n}^{\delta},\mathbf{w}_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{m}^{\delta}\in\Omega^{\delta}
be vertices in
\Omega^{\delta}
that are closest to points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}\in\Omega
in the complex plane.

We also define
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\gamma+\frac{3}{2}\log 2
, where
\gamma
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Theorem 4.2
.

Fix a positive constant
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
For
i=1,\ldots,n
, let
\bm{\varphi}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
be a local field with generalised scaling dimension
\Delta_{i}+\bar{\Delta}_{i}
.
Let
P_{i}^{\delta}\in\mathcal{P}
be a field-polynomial representative of the local field

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}_{i}\,-\,\log\big(\lambda\,\delta\big)\Big[({\mathbf{L}}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0})-(\Delta_{i}+\bar{\Delta}_{i})\Big]\bm{\varphi}_{i}\,.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{\Big\langle P_{1}^{\delta}\big(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\delta}\big)\cdots P_{n}^{\delta}\big(\mathbf{z}_{n}^{\delta}\big)\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}}{{{\delta}}^{\,\sum_{i}(\Delta_{i}+\bar{\Delta}_{i})}}\mspace{20mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{50mu}\delta\,\downarrow\,0\mspace{50mu}}\mspace{20mu}\Big\langle\bm{\varphi}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots\bm{\varphi}_{n}(z_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha(\lambda)}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

uniformly for
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
on compacts of
\textnormal{Conf}_{n}(\Omega)
, where we used
P_{i}^{\delta}\big(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{\delta}\big)\coloneqq\textnormal{ev}_{\mathbf{z}_{i}^{\delta}}^{\Omega^{\delta}}\big(P_{i}^{\delta}\big)
and
\alpha(\lambda)\coloneqq 2\big(\log\lambda+\mathsf{C}\,\big)
.

Remark 4.3
.

In each discrete approximation
\Omega^{\delta}
of the continuum domain
\Omega
, the linear scale of the system is proportional to
\delta
; the proportionality constant
\lambda
is arbitrary.
There is no good reason to pick as a linear scale the edge length
\delta
instead of the diagonal length of the square-grid plaquettes
\sqrt{2}\,\delta
or half the length of an edge
\delta/2
.

\diamond

Remark 4.4
.

This arbitrariness is
not
relevant in non-logarithmic theories—a change in the linear scale can be absorbed as a multiplicative factor in the local observables.
This is also the case for local fields in our theory with well-defined scaling dimensions.
However, our theory contains more exotic fields that feature logarithmic divergencies in the scaling limit.
These are the generalised eigenvectors of
{\mathbf{L}}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0}
that are not eigenvectors.

\diamond

In Section
2.1.2
, we gave an algebraic argument —the existence of x-trivial Virasoro self-isomorphisms of the space of the space of fields— to explain why symplectic-fermion correlation functions are defined only up to a choice of an arbitrary constant.
In this picture of the correlation functions of a logarithmic CFT —as the scaling limit of discrete correlation functions—, this fact becomes less abstract.
This ambiguity arises purely from the choice of an arbitrary linear scale; that is the choice of the arbitrary constant
\lambda
discussed in Remark
4.3
.
Let us see this in a specific example.

Example 4.5
.

The ground state
\bm{\upomega}
does not have a well-defined scaling dimension —recall that we have
({\mathbf{L}}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0})\bm{\upomega}=2\cdot\mathbf{1}
— which implies that it requires a logarithmic correction to get a non-trivial scaling limit.
More precisely, we have the convergence

\displaystyle\Big\langle\textnormal{ev}_{\mathbf{z}^{\delta}}^{\Omega^{\delta}}\Big(\bm{\upomega}-2\log(\lambda\,\delta)\mathbf{1}\Big)\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\mspace{20mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{50mu}\delta\,\downarrow\,0\mspace{50mu}}\mspace{20mu}\Big\langle\bm{\omega}(z)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha(\lambda)}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}=\,-\,4\pi\,\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)-\alpha(\lambda)

uniformly for
z
on compacts of
\Omega
.
The arbitrary CFT constant
\alpha
on the right-hand side arises from the arbitrary linear scale
\lambda
chosen in the discrete.

\diamond

We will see another example of this logarithmic behaviour in Section
4.3.2
below when we discuss the dissipation field of the Abelian sandpile model.
For general fields, we have the following formula that is reminiscent of the self-isomorphisms of the space
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
discussed in
[
Ada24
]
.

Example 4.6
.

For a field

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}\coloneqq\bm{\chi}_{-k_{r}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{s}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}}\bm{\upomega}

in the basis
(
4.1
)
, the corresponding field that behaves well under rescalings —in the sense of Theorem
4.2
— is

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}\,-\,\log\big(\lambda\,\delta\big)\Big[({\mathbf{L}}_{0}

\displaystyle+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0})-(\Delta+\bar{\Delta})\Big]\bm{\varphi}\,=

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{-k_{r}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{s}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{\bar{r}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-\bar{k}_{1}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{\bar{s}}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\bar{\ell}_{1}}\Big(\bm{\upomega}-2\,\log\big(\lambda\,\delta\big)\mathbf{1}\Big)

where
\Delta+\bar{\Delta}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}+\sum_{b=1}^{s}\ell_{b}+\sum_{\bar{a}=1}^{\bar{r}}\bar{k}_{\bar{a}}+\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{\bar{s}}\bar{\ell}_{\bar{b}}
.

\diamond

\begin{overpic}[./figures/wired_graph.pdf]
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.1:
On the left, a discrete (Jordan) domain
\Omega^{\delta}
and its boundary
\partial\Omega^{\delta}
(black)

in the infinite square grid
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
(gray).
On the right, its nearest-neighbour graph with

wired boundary
(V_{\Omega^{\delta}},E_{\Omega^{\delta}})
;
the thick line along the boundary vertices

indicates they all correspond to the same vertex in
V_{\Omega^{\delta}}
.

4.3Applications to probabilistic models

The results discussed above —Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
— concern purely the local fields of the fDGFF.
Yet, the fDGFF is known to be suitable to compute the correlation functions of certain observables of the uniform spanning tree and the Abelian sandpile model among other models.
We hence exploit this connection to understand (the scaling limit of) local observables in these models as local fields in the symplectic fermions CFT

To define our probabilistic models below we will need the following definitions.

For a discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
, consider its nearest-neighbour
graph with wired boundary
.
That is, the (multi)graph that one obtains by connecting with an edge all nearest neighbours in the domain
\Omega^{\delta}
and wiring all vertices in
\partial\Omega^{\delta}
into one single vertex while keeping track of possible double and triple edges near the boundary—see Figure
4.1
.
We let
(V_{\Omega^{\delta}},E_{\Omega^{\delta}})
denote this graph.

Let
e\in E_{\Omega^{\delta}}
be an edge.
If it is a horizontal edge, we let
\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{E};e}\in V_{\Omega^{\delta}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{W};e}\in V_{\Omega^{\delta}}
denote the vertices on the right and left of
e
, respectively.
Similarly, if
e
is a vertical edge, we let
\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{N};e}\in V_{\Omega^{\delta}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{S};e}\in V_{\Omega^{\delta}}
denote the vertices at the top and at the bottom of
e
, respectively.
Then, the
discrete gradient
of a function
f
on
\Omega^{\delta}
at the edge
e
is given by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\sharp}f(e)\,\coloneqq\,\begin{cases}\;f(\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{E};e})-f(\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{W};e})\phantom{\big|}&\mspace{20mu}\text{ if $e$ horizontal}\,,\\
\phantom{.}\\
\;f(\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{N};e})-f(\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{S};e})\phantom{\big|}&\mspace{20mu}\text{ if $e$ vertical}\,.\end{cases}

(4.2)

For functions of multiple variables, we use the notation
\big(\nabla_{\!\sharp}\big)_{(i)}
to indicate the gradient is taken with respect to the
i
-th variable.
For functions on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, we identify edges of the infinite square-grid with their midpoint seen as embedded in
{\mathbb{C}}
, and we use the notation

\nabla_{\!\sharp}f\big(k+\frac{1}{2}+\mathbbm{i}\ell\big)\coloneqq f(k+1+\mathbbm{i}\ell)-f(k+\mathbbm{i}\ell)

and

\nabla_{\!\sharp}f\big(k+\mathbbm{i}\ell+\frac{\mathbbm{i}}{2}\big)\coloneqq f(k+\mathbbm{i}\ell+\mathbbm{i})-f(k+\mathbbm{i}\ell)

for
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

4.3.1.The uniform spanning tree

A spanning tree of a graph is a subset of edges that contains no loops and covers every vertex.
Given a discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
, the
uniform spanning tree
(UST) on
\Omega^{\delta}
with wired boundary conditions is a spanning tree
\bm{\tau}
of
(V_{\Omega^{\delta}},E_{\Omega^{\delta}})
taken uniformly at random among all possible spanning trees.
We let
{\mathbb{P}}^{\text{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\text{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
denote, respectively, the UST probability measure and its expected values.
Then, the classical Kirchhoff’s matrix-tree theorem implies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{\text{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\bm{\tau}=T\,\big]\,=\,\frac{1}{\det(-\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}})}

for any wired spanning tree
T
of
\Omega^{\delta}
, where the matrix
\Delta_{\sharp}^{\texttt{D}}
with rows and columns indexed by the (interior) vertices of
\Omega^{\delta}
is the Dirichlet Laplacian matrix

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}})_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}}\,=\,\begin{cases}\mspace{15mu}-4&\mspace{30mu}\textnormal{if }\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{w}\,,\\
\mspace{15mu}\;\;1&\mspace{30mu}\textnormal{if }|\,\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{w}\,|=\delta\,,\textnormal{and}\\
\mspace{15mu}\;\;0&\mspace{30mu}\textnormal{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

as we defined in Section
3.1
.
By the celebrated transfer-current theorem of Burton and Pemantle —
[
BP93
]
— we have that the edges of the spanning tree form a determinantal process.
In precise terms, for any distinct edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{\text{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\in\bm{\tau}\big]\,=\,\det\bigg(\big(\nabla_{\!\sharp}\big)_{(1)}\big(\nabla_{\!\sharp}\big)_{(2)}\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}(e_{i},e_{j})\bigg)_{i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\,,

where
\mathbf{G}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
is the Dirichlet Green’s function as defined in Remark
3.1
.
Then, the following connection between the fDGFF and the UST follows from the transfer-current theorem and Remark
3.1
.

\begin{overpic}[./figures/UST.pdf]
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.2:
An example of a spanning tree with wired boundary conditions.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be distinct edges in
E_{\Omega^{\delta}}
.
We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{\textnormal{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\in\bm{\tau}\big]\,=\,\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n}}\Big\langle\nabla_{\!\sharp}\xi(e_{1})\nabla_{\!\sharp}\theta(e_{1})\cdots\nabla_{\!\sharp}\xi(e_{n})\nabla_{\!\sharp}\theta(e_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,.

This connection allows one to understand UST local observables as CFT fields in the scaling limit via our isomorphism —Theorem
4.1
—.
For concreteness we give two examples.

The degree field.

For a vertex
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}
, we define the degree of
\mathbf{z}
in the UST as the random variable
\mathsf{deg}(\mathbf{z})
whose value on each UST realisation is the number of open edges adjacent to the vertex
\mathbf{z}
—we refer to
\mathsf{deg}
as the
degree field
—see also
[
CCRR23
,
Rap23
]
.
From
[
CCRR23
, Corollary 3.4]
and Proposition
4.7
, it straightforwardly follows that we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\mathsf{deg}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{deg}(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\big]\;=\;\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n}}\,\Big\langle\sum_{e_{1}\sim\,\mathbf{z}_{1}}\!\nabla_{\!\sharp}\xi(e_{1})\nabla_{\!\sharp}\theta(e_{1})\cdots\sum_{e_{n}\sim\,\mathbf{z}_{n}}\!\nabla_{\!\sharp}\xi(e_{n})\nabla_{\!\sharp}\theta(e_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,,

where the notation
e\sim\mathbf{z}
indicated that the edge
e
contains the vertex
\mathbf{z}
.
However, these correlation functions have a trivial scaling limit; they converge to
2^{n}
, where
2
is the thermodynamic limit of the average of
\mathsf{deg}
.
A more interesting scaling limit is obtained if one considers the correlation functions of the difference of
\mathsf{deg}
from its average value.
Hence, we consider the fDGFF local field

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}\;\coloneqq\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\mspace{-20mu}\sum_{\mspace{30mu}x\in\{\pm 1,\pm\mathbbm{i}\}}\mspace{-20mu}\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\upxi}\big(\tfrac{x}{2}\big)\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}\big(\tfrac{x}{2}\big)\,-\,2\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

where we already subtracted the full-plane average of
\mathsf{deg}
—that is,
2
.

In order to take the scaling limit of correlation functions of the local field
\mathsf{T}
, we need to know its expression in the basis (
4.1
)—in Section
5.6
, we present an example of such a computation in detail.
For the degree field
\mathsf{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi}\big(\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-1}\bm{\eta}_{-1}+\bm{\chi}_{-1}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-1}\big)\mathbf{1}\mspace{15mu}+\parbox{100.00015pt}{\centering terms with higher scaling dimensions. \@add@centering}

In the scaling limit, the only relevant terms are those with the lowest scaling dimensions—higher scaling-dimension terms do not contribute to the scaling limit and can depend on the particular discretisation of the field theory.
These terms are mapped to the symplectic-fermions field
\mathbf{T}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\pi}({\upeta}_{-1}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-1}+{\upchi}_{-1}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-1})\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
via the isomorphism in Theorem
4.1
.
Hence, by Theorem
4.2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\big(\,\mathsf{deg}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta}_{1})-2\,\big)\cdots\big(\,\mathsf{deg}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta}_{n})-2\,\big)\big]}{\delta^{2\,n}}\mspace{10mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{20mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{20mu}}\mspace{10mu}\Big\langle\mathbf{T}(z_{1})\cdots\mathbf{T}(z_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

uniformly for
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
on compacts of
\textnormal{Conf}_{n}(\Omega)
.

Example 4.8
.

Let
\mathbf{z}^{\delta}
be the closest vertex to
z\in\Omega
in the discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\Big(\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\mathsf{deg}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta})\,\big]\,-\,2\,\Big)\mspace{10mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{20mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{20mu}}\mspace{10mu}\Big\langle\mathbf{T}(z)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,8\,\partial_{1}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{2}\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)

uniformly for
z
on compacts of
\Omega
.
Note that the limit is actually independent of
\alpha
.

\diamond

The (horizontal) open-edge field.

For a vertex
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega^{\delta}
,
let
\{\,\,\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}\text{ open at }\mathbf{z}\,\}

be the UST event that the edge adjacent to
\mathbf{z}
on its right is open and let
\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}(\mathbf{z})
denote its indicator function.
Similarly, let
\{\,\,\!\mathbf{-}\mspace{-7mu}\bullet\!\text{ open at }\mathbf{z}\,\}

be the UST event that the edge adjacent to the vertex
\mathbf{z}
on its left is open and let
\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}(\mathbf{z})
denote its indicator function.
The UST local observables
\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}(\mathbf{z})

and
\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}(\mathbf{z})

can be respectively associated with the fDGFF field polynomials

\displaystyle P_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}\;\coloneqq\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\upxi}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\mspace{30mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{30mu}P_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}\;\coloneqq\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\upxi}\big(\tfrac{-1\;}{2}\big)\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}\big(\tfrac{-1\;}{2}\big)\,.

in the sense that we have the equality —Proposition
4.7
—

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}(\mathbf{z}_{n})\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}(\mathbf{w}_{m})\,\big]\phantom{\bigg|}

\displaystyle\mspace{200mu}=\;\Big\langle\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}_{1}}\big(P_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}\big)\cdots\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}_{n}}\big(P_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}\big)\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{w}_{1}}\big(P_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}\big)\cdots\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{w}_{m}}\big(P_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}\big)\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,.

As we argued for the degree field, the more interesting local observables with non-constant correlation functions —with respect to the insertion point— in the scaling limit are

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}\,\longmapsto\,\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}(\mathbf{z})-\frac{1}{2}\mspace{40mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{40mu}\mathbf{z}\,\longmapsto\,\mathbbm{1}_{\,\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}(\mathbf{z})-\frac{1}{2}

where we have subtracted the thermodynamic limit of the probability of one given edge being open in the UST—that is,
\frac{1}{2}
.
As expected, their associated fields

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}\,\coloneqq\,P_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}-\frac{1}{2}+\textnormal{\small Null}\mspace{40mu}\textnormal{ and }\mspace{40mu}\mathsf{P}_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}\,\coloneqq\,P_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}-\frac{1}{2}+\textnormal{\small Null}

have the same leading-order terms in the basis (
4.1
).
In particular, we have that both
\mathsf{P}_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}
are equal to —see Section
5.6
—

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\big(\bm{\chi}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-1}\big)\big(\bm{\eta}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-1}\big)\mathbf{1}\mspace{15mu}+\parbox{100.00015pt}{\centering terms with higher scaling dimensions. \@add@centering}

We define then the fDGFF field

\mathsf{H}\coloneqq\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\big(\bm{\chi}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-1}\big)\big(\bm{\eta}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-1}\big)\mathbf{1}

and we let
\mathbf{H}\in\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
be its image via the isomorphism of Theorem
4.1
.

Example 4.9
.

Let
\mathbf{z}^{\delta}
be the closest vertex to
z\in\Omega
in the discrete domain
\Omega^{\delta}
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\bigg(\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\textnormal{UST}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\!\bullet\!\!\!\mathbf{-}\text{ open at }\mathbf{z}^{\delta}\,\big]\,-\,\frac{1}{2}\,\bigg)\mspace{10mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{20mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{20mu}}\mspace{10mu}\Big\langle\mathbf{H}(z)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,(\partial_{1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{1})(\partial_{2}+\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{2})\,\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)

uniformly for
z
on compacts of
\Omega
.
Note again that the limit does not depend on
\alpha
.

\diamond

An interesting observable is the difference
\mathsf{P}_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}-\mathsf{P}_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}
.
Within correlation functions, one can think of this observable as the horizontal derivative of either
\mathsf{P}_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}
or
\mathsf{P}_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}
since it is the difference of the open-edge field among two horizontally neighbouring edges.
In the basis (
4.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}-\mathsf{P}_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}\,=\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\Big[\big(\bm{\chi}_{-2}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-2}\big)\big(\bm{\eta}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-1}\big)\,+\,\big(\bm{\chi}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-1}\big)\big(\bm{\eta}_{-2}+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-2}\big)\Big]\mathbf{1}\,.

As expected from its physical interpretation, this field has scaling dimension
3
since it is the (horizontal) derivative of a field with scaling dimension
2
.

Using explicit formulae of the Sugawara construction —Lemma
2.2
—, it is straightforward to check that we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\bullet\!\mathbf{-}}-\mathsf{P}_{\!\mathbf{-}\!\bullet}\,=\,\big({\mathbf{L}}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{-1}\big)\,\mathsf{H}\,.

This fact is in agreement with the postulates of CFT:
the Virasoro modes
{\mathbf{L}}_{-1}
and
\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{-1}
behave, within correlation functions, as the holomorphic and antiholomorphic Wirtinger derivatives respectively—their linear combination
{\mathbf{L}}_{-1}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{-1}
corresponds then to the derivative in the horizontal direction.

4.3.2.The Abelian sandpile model

The model at hand
3
3

3

For simplicity, we consider here only the Abelian sandpile model with dissipative (or wired, or Dirichlet) boundary conditions.
was first introduced as a model for self-organized criticality in
[
BTW88
]
.
Sandpile models are typically defined via a Markov chain.
Consider the set
({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}
of positive-integer-valued height functions on the interior of
\Omega^{\delta}
.
We say two height functions
h,h^{\prime}\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}
are toppling-equivalent if they satisfy

\displaystyle h^{\prime}\,=\,h+\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}}n_{\mathbf{z}}T_{\mathbf{z}}

for some
n_{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where the
toppling

T_{\mathbf{z}}:^{\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
is defined via the Dirichlet Laplacian by

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{w})\,\coloneqq\,(\Delta_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\texttt{D}})_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}}\,.

The state space of the Markov chain is the set of height functions
({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{\Omega^{\delta}}
modulo topplings, which we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
.
One can see that each class of toppling-equivalent height functions has a unique representative in
\{1,2,3,4\}^{\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}}
;
we hence identify each class with this unique representative.
The dynamics of the Markov chain is then as follows:
at each discrete time step, the height function increases by
1
at a vertex selected uniformly at random among all the vertices in
\textnormal{int}\,{\Omega^{\delta}}
.

The unique stationary measure of this Markov chain
{\mathbb{P}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\text{ASM}}
is uniform on the set
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
of recurrent states —
[
Dha90
]
—.
We let
{\mathbb{E}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\text{ASM}}
denote the expectation value with respect to this probability measure.

The
Abelian sandpile model
is then the probabilistic model whose space of configurations is
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
equipped with the uniform measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\text{ASM}}
.

\begin{overpic}[./figures/ASM.pdf]
\put(93.0,35.6){\footnotesize$1$}
\put(93.0,32.9){\footnotesize$2$}
\put(93.0,30.2){\footnotesize$3$}
\put(93.0,27.5){\footnotesize$4$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.3:
A configuration of the Abelian sandpile model with wired

boundary conditions in a discrete domain.
This configuration corresponds

to the spanning tree in Figure
4.2
via Dhar’s burning algorithm.

It is noteworthy that there is a non-local coupling —that is, a bijection that preserves measures— between the ASM and the UST known as the
burning algorithm
—
[
MaDh92
]
—.
The (scaling limit of the) Abelian sandpile model is also conjectured to be described by a logarithmic CFT with central charge
-2
; see
[
Rue21
]
for a recent review.
Though the scaling-limit CFT is predicted not to be equivalent to the symplectic fermions, certain (local) fields in these two distinct logCFTs can be put in correspondence.

The height-one field.

Consider the observable

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}\;\longmapsto\;\mathbbm{1}_{\{h(\mathbf{z})=1\}}

which takes values
1
on ASM configurations in which the height at
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\delta}
is
1
and takes the value
0
otherwise.
Its full-plane average —computed for the first time in
[
MaDh91
]
— is
\frac{2(\pi-2)}{\pi^{3}}
, and its fluctuations around this average have been argued to be described by a local field in the symplectic fermions theory —
[
MaRu01
]
—.

By means of the fDGFF —
[
CCRR23
, Theorem 3.5]
—, we can compute the correlation functions

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{h(\mathbf{z}_{1})=1\}}\cdots\mathbbm{1}_{\{h(\mathbf{z}_{n})=1\}}\,\big]\;=\;\Big\langle H_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots H_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}

with the fDGFF field-polynomial

\displaystyle H_{1}(\mathbf{z})\,\coloneqq\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\Bigg[\,\prod_{e\sim\mathbf{z}}\Big(\nabla_{\!\sharp}\xi(e)\nabla_{\!\sharp}\theta(e)-1\Big)-1\,\Bigg]\,.

We hence define the local observable
\mathsf{h}_{1}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z})\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\{h(\mathbf{z})=1\}}-\frac{2(\pi-2)}{\pi^{3}}\,,

which is called the
height-one field
.
Its associated fDGFF local field is

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{1}\,\coloneqq\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\Bigg[\prod_{x\in\{\pm 1,\pm\mathbbm{i}\}}\!\!\Big(\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\upxi}\big(\tfrac{x}{2}\big)\nabla_{\!\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}\big(\tfrac{x}{2}\big)-1\Big)-1\,\Bigg]-\frac{2\,(\pi-2)}{\pi^{3}}\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,,

which, in terms of the CFT basis of the logarithmic Fock space —Theorem
4.1
— is written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{1}\;=\;-\frac{\pi-2}{\pi^{3}}\,\Big(\,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-1}\bm{\eta}_{-1}+\bm{\chi}_{-1}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-1}\Big)\mathbf{1}\mspace{15mu}+\parbox{100.00015pt}{\centering terms with higher scaling dimensions.\@add@centering}

We refer the reader to Section
5.6
to see an explicit example of such a computation.

We also define
\mathbf{H}_{1}\coloneqq-\frac{\pi-2}{\pi^{2}}\big(\,\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\upchi$}}}}\upchi_{-1}{\upeta}_{-1}+{\upchi}_{-1}\mathrlap{\hskip-0.6pt\overline{\scalebox{0.97}[1]{\phantom{$\upeta$}}}}\upeta_{-1}\big)\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
in the logarithmic Fock space
\mathscr{F}{\!\!\textnormal{\footnotesize ock}}
—the image of
\mathsf{H}_{1}
via the isomorphism in Theorem
4.1
.
This local field coincides with the one predicted in
[
MaRu01
]
.

Example 4.10
.

By Theorem
4.2
, we have the following scaling-limit convergence of the one-point function:

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}\big[\,\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta})\,\big]}{\delta^{2}}\mspace{10mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{30mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{30mu}}\mspace{10mu}\Big\langle\mathbf{H}_{1}(z)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}\,=\,-\frac{8\,(\pi-2)}{\pi^{2}}\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)

uniformly for
z
on compacts of
\Omega
, where
\mathbf{z}^{\delta}
is the vertex in
\Omega^{\delta}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
that is closest to
z
.
Note the independence with respect to the constant
\alpha
.

\diamond

The dissipation field.

Another interesting observable in this model is the insertion of dissipative sites in the bulk, that is, sites that do not topple.
This observable was fit into the CFT description of the ASM as a logarithmic local field in
[
PiRu04
]
—we should revisit these ideas here.

The dissipation field is
not
probabilistic in nature, but it rather modifies the configuration space of the ASM.
In precise terms, the configurations of the ASM on
\Omega^{\delta}
with dissipative sites at the vertices
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n}\in\Omega^{\delta}
are the configurations of the ASM on
\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n}\}
.

Let
\mathbf{W}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n}\}
denote the set of dissipative sites in the bulk.
For a random variable
X
of the ASM on
\Omega^{\delta}
that is determined by the values of
h|_{\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\mathbf{W}}
, we introduce the following notation for expectations with dissipative sites:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,X\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{n})\,\big]\,\coloneqq\,\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}_{\Omega^{\delta}}|}\mspace{-30mu}\sum_{\mspace{50mu}h\in\mathcal{R}_{\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\mathbf{W}}}\mspace{-30mu}X(h)\,.

Note the sum runs over ASM configurations on
\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\mathbf{W}
, but the normalising constant is the number of ASM configurations on
\Omega^{\delta}
.
We refer to
\mathsf{d}
as the
dissipation field
.

Example 4.11
.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,1\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{n})\,\big]\,=\,\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\mathbf{W}}|}{|\mathcal{R}_{\Omega^{\delta}}|}\,.

where we let
1
denote the constant random variable with constant value
1
.

\diamond

We also define
{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\,X\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.55}{\hskip-2.0pt { ~- - - }}}\,1\,]\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[\,X\,]
and extend linearly in the following sense:
given the vertices
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{\ell}\in\Omega^{\delta}
and the constants
\alpha,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
we define

\displaystyle\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,X\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\bigg(\alpha\,+\,\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{j}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{x}_{j})\bigg)\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{n})\,\big]\,\coloneqq\,

\displaystyle\mspace{100mu}\phantom{\bigg|}\alpha\cdot{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,X\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{n})\,\big]\,+\,\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\Big(\alpha_{j}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,X\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{x}_{j})\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{n})\,\big]\Big)\,.

The following result —whose proof we postpone to Section
5.5
— allows one to compute the correlation functions of an arbitrary number of height-one fields in the presence of an arbitrary number of dissipation fields.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n},\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{m}\in\Omega^{\delta}
be interior vertices of
\Omega^{\delta}
.
We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\big[\,\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}_{m})\,\big]\,=\,\Big\langle H_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdots H_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{n})D(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots D(\mathbf{w}_{m})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}

with
D(\mathbf{z})\coloneqq 1+\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\xi(\mathbf{z})\theta(\mathbf{z})
.

It is straightforward to see —by the definitions in Example
3.8
— that, in the basis (
4.1
), the fDGFF field
\mathsf{D}\coloneqq 1+\frac{1}{4\pi}\bm{\upxi}(0)\bm{\uptheta}(0)+\textnormal{\small Null}
associated to the dissipation field is written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}\,=\,\mathbf{1}\,-\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\bm{\upomega}\,,

which is a field with generalised scaling dimensions
0
.
However, we argue that the term of the logarithmic field
\bm{\upomega}
is the only relevant term to understand the physical behaviour of this field.
To see this, consider the statement of Theorem
4.2
applied to the particular case of the correlation function in Lemma
4.12
.

On the one hand, the height-one field was established to be a primary field with scaling dimension
2
—no logarithmic correction is involved in the scaling limit of this field.
As for the dissipation field, a field-polynomial representative of the field
\mathsf{D}-\log(\lambda\,\delta)\big[({\mathbf{L}}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0})-0\,\big]\mathsf{D}
is

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{4\pi}\bm{\upxi}(0)\bm{\uptheta}(0)\,+\,\frac{\log(\lambda\,\delta)}{2\pi}\,.

Yet, this is also a field-polynomial representative of
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\bm{\upomega}-\log(\tilde{\lambda}\,\delta)\big[({\mathbf{L}}_{0}+\mathrlap{\hskip-1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.85}[1]{\phantom{$\mathbf{L}$}}}}\mathbf{L}_{0})-0\,\big]\big(-\frac{1}{4\pi}\bm{\upomega}\big)
with a different arbitrary scale constant
\tilde{\lambda}\coloneqq e^{-4\pi}\lambda
.
According to Theorem
4.2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{{{\delta}}}^{2\cdot m}}\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\Big[\,\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta}_{1})\cdots\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta}_{m})\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\Big(\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}^{\delta}_{1})+\tfrac{\log(\lambda\,\delta)}{2\pi}\Big)\cdots\Big(\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}^{\delta}_{n})+\tfrac{\log(\lambda\,\delta)}{2\pi}\Big)\,\Big]\phantom{\Bigg|}

\displaystyle\mspace{210mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{50mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{50mu}}\mspace{3mu}\phantom{\Bigg|}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(4\pi)^{n}}\,\Big\langle\,\mathbf{H}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots\mathbf{H}_{1}(z_{m})\,\bm{\omega}(w_{1})\cdots\bm{\omega}(w_{n})\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha(\tilde{\lambda})}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

uniformly for
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
on compacts of
\textnormal{Conf}_{n+m}(\Omega)
.

Note, once more, that the scaling constant
\lambda
is arbitrary, and the scaling limit is unique only up to a choice of this constant.

Example 4.13
.

For one height-one field and one dissipation field, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{{{\delta}}}^{2}}\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\textnormal{ASM}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}\Big[\,\mathsf{h}_{1}(\mathbf{z}^{\delta})\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.65}{{ ~- - - }}}\,\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{w}^{\delta})+\tfrac{\log(\lambda\,\delta)}{2\pi}\,\Big]\mspace{10mu}\xrightarrow{\mspace{20mu}\delta\downarrow 0\mspace{20mu}}\mspace{10mu}\phantom{\Bigg|}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\Big\langle\,\mathbf{H}_{1}(z)\,\bm{\omega}(w)\Big\rangle_{\Omega;\alpha(\tilde{\lambda})}^{\textnormal{SyFe}}

\displaystyle\mspace{140mu}\phantom{\bigg|}=\,\frac{8\,(\pi-2)\,}{\pi^{2}}\,\Big(\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,z)\big[\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(w,w)+\alpha(\tilde{\lambda})\big]+\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{1}\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,w)\,\partial_{1}\mathbf{g}_{\Omega}(z,w)\Big)

uniformly for
(z,w)
in compacts of
\textnormal{Conf}_{2}(\Omega)
,
where
\mathbf{z}^{\delta}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\delta}
are the vertices in
\Omega^{\delta}
that are closest to
z
and
w
respectively.

\diamond

5Proofs

We now present the proofs that were omitted in the discussion of our results.

In Sections
5.1
and
5.2
we elaborate on preliminaries necessary for the proof of Theorem
4.1
, which is presented in Section
5.3
.
In Section
5.4
, we give the proof of Theorem
4.2
.
Finally, Section
5.5
is devoted to the proof of Lemma
4.12
regarding the dissipation field of the Abelian sandpile model.
In the final Section
5.6
, we give the details of one computation for expressing a local field in terms of the BPZ basis of the Fock space.

5.1Linear local fields

Before handling all fDGFF local fields, we consider a simple subspace of local fields that can be characterised more easily.
This subspace is the set of linear local fields, that is, fields that have a homogeneous field-polynomial representative of degree
1
.
The key insight is that one can build the rest of local fields via a certain product —the normally-ordered product— of linear local fields; this idea is developed in Section
5.2
.

We define the space of
linear field polynomials
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}\,\coloneqq\,\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\big\{\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}),\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\,\colon\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\big\}\,,

which is a vector subspace of
\mathcal{P}
.
Then, the space of
linear local fields
is the quotient

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\textnormal{lin}}\,\coloneqq\,\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}/\mathcal{N}\,.

Example 5.1
.

The fields

\displaystyle\bm{\upxi}\,\coloneqq\,\bm{\upxi}(0)\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\mspace{60mu}\text{and}\mspace{60mu}\bm{\uptheta}\,\coloneqq\,\bm{\uptheta}(0)\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

transparently belong in
\mathcal{F}_{\textnormal{lin}}
.
We define their representatives
{X}_{0}\coloneqq\bm{\upxi}(0)
and
{H}_{0}\coloneqq\bm{\uptheta}(0)
.

\diamond

Example 5.2
.

The fields

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}^{[-k]}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,\eqqcolon\,{X}_{-k}\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}}\partial_{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{u}}_{\textnormal{m}}^{[-k]}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,\eqqcolon\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{${X}$}}}}{X}_{-k}\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial_{\sharp}\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}^{[-k]}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,\eqqcolon\,{H}_{-k}\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,,\mspace{20mu}\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{\textnormal{m}}}\partial_{\sharp}\overline{\mathbf{u}}_{\textnormal{m}}^{[-k]}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.8}[1]{\phantom{$\partial$}}}}\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,\eqqcolon\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{${H}$}}}}{H}_{-k}\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}

are linear local fields.
That is, they have linear field polynomial representatives.

\diamond

Lemma 5.3
.

The set

\displaystyle\big\{\bm{\chi}_{-k}\mathbf{1},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k}\mathbf{1},\bm{\eta}_{-k}\mathbf{1},\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-k}\mathbf{1},\,\colon k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}\big\}\,\cup\,\big\{\bm{\uptheta},\bm{\upxi}\big\}

constitutes a basis of the space
\mathcal{F}_{\textnormal{lin}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same lines as the proof of Theorem 5.1 in
[
ABK24
]
with the addition of the non-zero average linear fields
\bm{\uptheta}
and
\bm{\upxi}
.
∎

In a similar fashion as in
[
ABK24
]
the previous lemma leads to a complete classification of the linear null fields of the fDGFF.

Corollary 5.4
.

The set

\displaystyle\big\{\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\upxi}(\mathbf{u}),\Delta_{\sharp}\bm{\uptheta}(\mathbf{u})\,\colon\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\big\}

spans the subspace
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}
of null linear local field polynomials.

5.2Higher-degree local fields

We now define a way of obtaining higher-degree field polynomials as a normally-ordered product of linear field polynomials,
which we have completely characterised in Section
5.1
.
To address this task, we need the
full-plane square-grid Green’s function
—
[
LaLi10
]
—,
that is, the unique function
\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

that satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{\sharp}\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}(\mathbf{u})\,=\,-\delta_{\mathbf{u},0}\mspace{20mu}\text{ and }\mspace{20mu}\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}(0)\,=\,0\,,

and has the asymptotic behaviour

\displaystyle\mspace{150mu}\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\,=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\mathbf{u}|-\frac{\mathsf{C}}{2\pi}+\mathcal{O}\big(|\mathbf{u}|^{-2}\big)\mspace{50mu}\text{ as }\mspace{5mu}|\mathbf{u}|\to\infty\,,

with
\mathsf{C}=\gamma+\frac{3}{2}\log 2
, where
\gamma
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Via the full plane Green’s function
\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
, we define the
Wick contractions
of linear field polynomials

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}\;\longrightarrow\;

\displaystyle\,{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle L_{1}\otimes L_{2}\;\longmapsto\;

\displaystyle\,\hbox to6.81pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\diamond

Normal ordering
is a linear map
\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
.
However, we conceive it as a linear map

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\cdots\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\colon\bigoplus_{d=0}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}\big)^{\otimes d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}

by the natural identification of
\bigoplus_{d=0}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}\big)^{\otimes d}
with
\mathcal{P}
.
It is defined as follows:
for linear field polynomials
L_{1},\cdots,L_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}
,
we define

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{1}\cdots L_{n}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\coloneqq\sum_{P\in\mathfrak{P}_{n}}(-1)^{P}(-1)^{|P|}\prod_{(i,j)\in P}\hbox to6.81pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.40279pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{i}\hbox to6.81pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.40279pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 8.88 L 0 13.26 L 0 13.26 L 0 8.88}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{j}\vbox to13.00002pt{}{}\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\cup P}^{\rightarrow}L_{k}

where
\mathfrak{P}_{n}
is the set of (ordered)
partial pairings
of the set
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, that is, an element of this set is
P=\big\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{|P|},j_{|P|})\big\}
with
i_{p}<j_{p}
and such that the set
\cup P\coloneqq\{i_{1},j_{1},\ldots,i_{|P|},j_{|P|}\}
is of order
2|P|
.
We also write
[n]\setminus\cup P=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-|P|}\}
with
k_{\alpha}<k_{\beta}
for
\alpha<\beta
.
Then, we let
(-1)^{P}
denote the signature of the permutation
(1\;2\;\cdots\;n)\mapsto(i_{1}\ j_{1}\,\cdots\,i_{|P|}\ j_{|P|}\ k_{1}\,\cdots\,k_{n-|P|})
.
Finally, the notation
\prod^{\rightarrow}
indicates the product is taken in increasing order of the indices, that is, we have

\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\cup P}^{\rightarrow}L_{k}=L_{k_{1}}\,L_{k_{2}}\cdots L_{k_{n-|P|}}
.

.

Remark 5.7
.

The normal ordering of
n>2
linear field polynomials

can be inductively proven to satisfy the formula

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{n}\,L_{n-1}\cdots L_{1}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\,=\,L_{n}\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{n-1}\cdots L_{1}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}\,\hbox to6.81pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.40279pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 8.88 L 0 13.26 L 0 13.26 L 0 8.88}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{n-i}\vbox to13.00002pt{}{}\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{n-1}\cdots\widehat{L}_{n-i}\cdots L_{1}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\,,

where the hat (
\,\widehat{\phantom{m}}\;\!
) indicates the factor is not in the product.

\diamond

Remark 5.8
.

The map
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\cdot\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\colon\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
is surjective.
To see this, note that, for a field polynomial
P
of degree
d
, the field polynomial
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,P\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,-P
is of degree at most
d-2
.
That is, the map
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\cdot\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,
is upper triangular with
1
s on the diagonal.

\diamond

Remark 5.9
.

Let
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}
be linear field polynomials.
If
L_{i}
is null for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, then
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{1}\cdots L_{n}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,
is a null field polynomial too.
To prove this, one first needs to expand the expression of the correlation function

\displaystyle\Big\langle\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}}\big(\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{1}\cdots L_{n}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\big)\,\xi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{1})\cdots\xi(\mathbf{z}_{m})\,\theta(\mathbf{w}_{m})\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}

by means of Wick’s formula —Remark
3.1
— and the definition of normal ordering.
The proof eventually boils down to the observation that we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\textnormal{ev}^{\Omega^{\delta}}_{\mathbf{z}}\big(\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{1}L_{2}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\big)\,\Big\rangle_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{fDGFF}}\,=\,0

whenever
L_{1}
or
L_{2}
are null linear fields—recall the characterisation of null linear fields in Corollary
5.4
and the Example
5.6
.
We refer the reader to
[
ABK24
, Lemma 6.2]
for a detailed proof of this statement in the bosonic setting.

\diamond

In other words, Remark
5.9
asserts that the map
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\cdots\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,
factors through the quotient with null fields.
In particular, it means that,
for
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{\textnormal{lin}}
linear local fields with linear representatives
L_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{lin}}
—that is, we have
F_{i}=L_{i}+\textnormal{\small Null}
—,
the map

\displaystyle F_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{n}\;\,\longmapsto\;\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,L_{1}\,\cdots\,L_{n}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\,+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,\eqqcolon\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,F_{1}\cdots F_{n}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,

is well-defined, that is, the normally ordered product does not depend on the choice of linear representatives.

The following lemma is the last piece we need for the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
Given the basis (
4.1
), it essentially asserts that all local fields in
\mathcal{F}
can be constructed as the normally-ordered product of enough linear fields.

Lemma 5.10
.

Denote by
T
the tensor product

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bm{\chi}_{-k_{n}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bm{\eta}_{-{\ell}_{m}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{\overline{n}}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{1}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{\overline{m}}}\mathbf{1}

of linear fields.
Then we have

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,T\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{n}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-{\ell}_{m}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{\overline{n}}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{\overline{m}}}\mathbf{1}\,,

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,T\otimes{\bm{\uptheta}}\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{n}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-{\ell}_{m}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{\overline{n}}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{\overline{m}}}\bm{\uptheta}\,

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,T\otimes{\bm{\upxi}}\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{n}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-{\ell}_{m}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{\overline{n}}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{\overline{m}}}\bm{\upxi}\,,\mspace{15mu}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,T\otimes{\bm{\uptheta}\otimes\bm{\upxi}}\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,

\displaystyle\phantom{\Big|}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{n}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{1}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-{\ell}_{m}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\chi}$}}}}\bm{\chi}_{-k_{\overline{n}}}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{1}}\cdots\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{1}[1]{\phantom{$\bm{\eta}$}}}}\bm{\eta}_{-\overline{\ell}_{\overline{m}}}\bm{\upomega}\,.

However, in the proof of this lemma, we use the following rather technical result regarding the linear local fields —rather, their representatives— in Examples
5.2
and
5.1
.

Proof.

We prove the case
\ell=0
for general
k>0
.
Recall that we defined
X_{0}=\bm{\upxi}(0)
in Example
5.1
.
We have

\displaystyle\sqint_{\gamma}\mathbf{u}_{\diamond}^{[-k]}\,\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\hbox to4.69pt{\vbox to6.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.34721pt\lower-3.47221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.34721pt}{-3.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\hbox to9.07pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.53471pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.53471pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 8.96 L 0 13.18 L 0 13.26 L 0 8.88}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{-\ell}\vbox to13.00002pt{}{}\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\,=\,-4\pi\sqint_{\gamma}\mathbf{u}_{\diamond}^{[-k]}\,\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}(\mathbf{u}_{\textnormal{m}})\,\textnormal{d}^{\sharp}\mathbf{u}\,.

Note that, away from the origin, both integrands are discrete holomorphic, so the above integral does not depend on the (large enough) discrete contour
\gamma
.
Recall that, as
|\mathbf{u}|\to\infty
, we have
\mathbf{u}^{[-k]}=\mathcal{O}\big(|\mathbf{u}|^{-k}\big)
and
\partial^{\bullet}_{\sharp}\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}(\mathbf{u})=\mathcal{O}\big(|\mathbf{u}|^{-1}\big)
.
Let
\gamma(R)
be a discrete contour with a square shape of sidelength
R
.
The above integral has the assymptotic behaviour
\mathcal{O}\big(R^{-k}\big)
as
R\to\infty
, but, since it does not depend on
R
, taking the limit
R\to\infty
we see that it must vanish.
The case
\ell>0
can be handled by similar arguments—we refer the reader to
[
ABK24
, Lemma 6.5]
for the details of these arguments in the bosonic setting.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.10
.

We write down the details only for the case
\overline{n}=\overline{m}=0
;
the proof in the general case follows similar arguments.
We proceed by induction on
n+m
.
The linear base cases

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\bm{\chi}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,\bm{\chi}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,,\mspace{40mu}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\bm{\eta}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,\bm{\eta}_{-k}\mathbf{1}\,,\mspace{40mu}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\bm{\upxi}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,\bm{\upxi}\,,\mspace{20mu}\text{ and }\mspace{20mu}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\bm{\uptheta}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,\bm{\uptheta}

are straightforward to check since normal ordering
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\cdots\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,
is the identity map when restricted to linear field polynomials.
The value
\mathbf{G}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}(0)=0
implies that we also have

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\bm{\uptheta}\otimes\bm{\upxi}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,\,=\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\bm{\uptheta}(0)\bm{\upxi}(0)\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,+\textnormal{\small Null}\,=\,\bm{\uptheta}(0)\bm{\upxi}(0)+\,\textnormal{\small Null}\,=\,\bm{\upomega}\,.

For the rest of the proof we focus only on the last case —that is, the
\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,T\otimes\bm{\uptheta}\otimes\bm{\upxi}\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \circ\,\circ$}}\,
case—, the other three can be handled in a similar manner.
By the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{-k_{n}}\cdots\bm{\chi}_{-k_{1}}\bm{\eta}_{-\ell_{m}}\cdots\bm{\eta}_{-{\ell}_{1}}\bm{\upomega}=\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,F\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,+\textnormal{\small Null}

with

F\,\coloneqq\,{X}_{-k_{n}}\cdots{X}_{-k_{1}}{H}_{-\ell_{m}}\cdots{H}_{-\ell_{1}}{H}_{0}\,{X}_{0}
,
where
{X}_{k}
and
{H}_{\ell}
are the field-polynomial representatives of the linear fields in Examples
5.1
and
5.2
.
In what follows, we also use the notation

\displaystyle F_{0}^{\hat{H}}

\displaystyle\;\coloneqq\,{X}_{-k_{n}}\cdots{X}_{-k_{1}}{H}_{-\ell_{m}}\cdots{H}_{-\ell_{1}}{X}_{0}\,,\mspace{20mu}\text{ and }

\displaystyle F_{j}^{\hat{H}}

\displaystyle\;\coloneqq\,{X}_{-k_{n}}\cdots{X}_{-k_{1}}{H}_{-\ell_{m}}\cdots\widehat{{H}}_{-\ell_{j}}\cdots{H}_{-\ell_{1}}{H}_{0}\,{X}_{0}\,,

where the hat (
\,\widehat{\phantom{m}}\;\!
) indicates the factor is not in the product.
We prove the inductive step for the induction in
n
—the induction in
m
can be handled almost identically.
Then, on the one hand, using the recursive formula in by Remark
5.7
, we have

\displaystyle\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,{X}_{-k_{n+1}}F\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,\;=\;{X}_{-k_{n+1}}\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,F\,\,\rotatebox[]{90.0}{\scalebox{.8}{$\ \bullet\,\bullet$}}\,
